Differential testing with random programs found over \textbf{325 previously unknown bugs} in GCC, LLVM and commercial C compilers in three years (\textbf{Csmith}: Yang, Chen, Eide, Regehr, PLDI 2011; every compiler tested crashed and miscompiled valid input at least once; in the verified passes of CompCert no wrong-code bug was found); \textbf{YARPGen} (Livinskii, Babokin, Regehr, OOPSLA 2020) reported more than 220 defects in GCC, LLVM and Intel's compiler; \textbf{CLsmith} (Lidbury, Lascu, Chong, Donaldson, PLDI 2015) found bugs in every OpenCL device/compiler configuration it tested (21 in the final version, from CPUs, GPUs and other accelerators).
\end{srcbox}

\section{Generating test programs \prio{3}}
\begin{itemize}[nosep]
\item \textbf{Defined behaviour by construction} (the hardest requirement): initialise everything; guard or wrap arithmetic (\texttt{safe\_div}, unsigned arithmetic for wrap-around, masked shift counts); bounded loops; no out-of-range indices; no pointer tricks; for GPUs, \emph{race freedom} (each thread writes only its own output slot, barriers only in block-uniform control flow, no float atomics whose order matters).
\item \textbf{Deterministic and self-reporting:} no clocks, no addresses, fixed seeds; end the program by printing a \emph{checksum} of all state, so that any difference is visible with one comparison.
\item \textbf{Diversity beyond uniform randomness:} YARPGen's \emph{generation policies} bias the generator towards constructs that make optimisers fire (re-using sub-expressions, loop nests with known trip counts, copy chains); Chapter~\ref{ch:lab} shows how much a policy matters.
\item \textbf{Cheap and reducible:} millions of programs per night, each small enough to reduce automatically.
\end{itemize}
\begin{center}\small
\begin{xltabular}{\textwidth}{@{}L{3.1cm}L{3.2cm}Y@{}}
\toprule
\textbf{Tool} & \textbf{Target} & \textbf{Idea}\\
\midrule\endhead
\textbf{Csmith} & C & UB-free random C with a global checksum; static analysis keeps programs valid\\
\textbf{YARPGen} & C, C++ (loops, SIMD) & UB avoided \emph{without} run-time checks; generation policies increase optimiser coverage\\
\textbf{CLsmith} & OpenCL kernels (GPU) & Csmith-style modes for many-core code plus an EMI mode; found bugs in every OpenCL device/compiler configuration tested\\
\textbf{GraphicsFuzz} / \textbf{spirv-fuzz} & GLSL/SPIR-V shaders & metamorphic transformations of real shaders (Donaldson et al., OOPSLA 2017); several driver compilers affected\\
\textbf{llvm-stress}, \textbf{libFuzzer}-based fuzzers (\texttt{clang-fuzzer}, \texttt{llvm-isel-fuzzer}) & LLVM IR, source, instruction selection & random or coverage-guided inputs aimed at \emph{crashes} and verifier failures rather than wrong code\\
\textbf{Learning-based} (DeepSmith, NNSmith) & OpenCL, deep-learning compilers & language models or graph generators trained on real code/models; weaker validity guarantees, new input shapes\\
\bottomrule
\end{xltabular}
\end{center}
Crash-testing LLVM's back ends with random IR takes ten lines of shell. We feed \texttt{llvm-stress} modules to \texttt{llc} for four targets (including NVPTX, the open-source PTX back end) with the machine verifier enabled, and classify every outcome as \emph{ok}, \emph{diagnosed} (a clean error message: fine) or \emph{crash} (signal, assertion, timeout: a bug):
\Needspace*{5\baselineskip}
\begin{lstlisting}[language={bash},basicstyle=\ttfamily\scriptsize,title={stress.sh}]
#!/bin/bash
# stress.sh N   crash-test several LLVM back ends with random IR from llvm-stress; classify every outcome
N=${1:-100}
printf "%-22s %6s %10s %8s\n" "llc target" "ok" "diagnosed" "crash"
for target in "-march=x86-64" "-march=aarch64" "-march=riscv64" "-march=nvptx64 -mcpu=sm_80"; do
  ok=0; err=0; crash=0
  for seed in $(seq 1 $N); do
    llvm-stress -seed=$seed -size=300 -o /tmp/s.ll 2>/dev/null
    timeout 20 llc -O2 -verify-machineinstrs $target /tmp/s.ll -o /dev/null >/tmp/s.out 2>&1; rc=$?
    if [ $rc -eq 0 ]; then ok=$((ok+1))
    elif [ $rc -ge 124 ]; then crash=$((crash+1)); echo "seed $seed: $(grep -m1 -E 'Stack dump|Assertion|LLVM ERROR' /tmp/s.out)" >> crashes.txt
    else err=$((err+1)); fi
  done
  printf "%-22s %6d %10d %8d\n" "$(echo $target | sed 's/-march=//')" $ok $err $crash
done
\end{lstlisting}
\begin{lstlisting}[style=shcmd,basicstyle=\ttfamily\scriptsize]
$ ./stress.sh 40
\end{lstlisting}
\begin{lstlisting}[style=out,basicstyle=\ttfamily\scriptsize]
llc target                 ok  diagnosed    crash
x86-64                     40          0        0
aarch64                    40          0        0
riscv64                    40          0        0
nvptx64 -mcpu=sm_80        40          0        0
\end{lstlisting}
\textbf{Zero crashes is the normal result} for a mature back end and a generator that has run for years: the infrastructure (classification, seed logging, timeouts) is the product, and a campaign that finds nothing is evidence, not failure. New bugs come from \emph{new kinds of input} (new generators, new instructions, new architectures such as a new \texttt{sm\_} target) --- which is exactly the situation a team that ships a new GPU generation every two years is in.

\subsection*{Designing a generator for GPU kernels (a favourite whiteboard question)}
Each thread owns one output element (\texttt{out[gid] = \textbackslash{}ldots}), so there are no races; the program is a straight-line or bounded-loop expression DAG over \emph{integer} arithmetic with safe-math wrappers (division guarded, shifts masked, signed overflow avoided by computing in \texttt{unsigned}); floating point only as exactly-representable values or with a tolerance; barriers appear only at points that every thread reaches, with shared-memory phases (write, \texttt{\_\_syncthreads()}, read neighbour); divergence is created by conditions on \texttt{threadIdx} and on data; the host compares a checksum against a CPU run of the \emph{same} source (the dual-compilation trick of Chapter~\ref{ch:cuda}), then across \texttt{-O0}/\texttt{-O3}, across \texttt{sm\_} targets and across compiler versions.

\section{Metamorphic testing and EMI \prio{2}}
When there is no second implementation, make the program its own oracle: apply a transformation whose effect on behaviour is known (``must be identical'') and compare the two runs of the \emph{same} compiler. \textbf{Equivalence Modulo Inputs} (EMI; Le, Afshari, Su, PLDI 2014) runs a program on an input, finds code that was \emph{not executed}, and deletes or mutates it: the variants must behave identically \emph{on that input}; a difference means the optimiser used a fact derived from dead code. The authors' tool \emph{Orion} reported 147 confirmed GCC and LLVM bugs in eleven months, mostly miscompilations.
\begin{center}\small
\begin{xltabular}{\textwidth}{@{}L{4.4cm}Y@{}}
\toprule
\textbf{Relation (``must not change the output'')} & \textbf{What it stresses}\\
\midrule\endhead
delete or insert code that is dead for the chosen input (EMI) & constant propagation, jump threading, alias facts, loop optimisations\\
replace \texttt{e} by \texttt{e+0}, \texttt{1*e}, \texttt{-(-e)}, \texttt{e-0} & algebraic simplification, instruction selection\\
wrap statements in \texttt{if (true-but-opaque)}; add an always-false branch & control-flow clean-up, branch folding, CFG shape\\
rename variables, reorder independent statements or functions & value numbering, scheduling, register allocation\\
compile with different but equivalent flags (e.g.\ vectoriser on/off) & the optimiser pipeline itself\\
\textbf{GPU:} change the block size for a block-size-independent kernel; permute blocks; pad inputs; split one kernel into two & launch-configuration assumptions, shared-memory sizing, barrier placement\\
\bottomrule
\end{xltabular}
\end{center}
\textbf{Limit:} a bug that damages the base program and every variant \emph{in the same way} stays invisible (a ``common-mode'' failure); Chapter~\ref{ch:lab} measures exactly this: two seeded bugs that differential testing catches immediately are never seen by the metamorphic checker.

\section{Test-case reduction \prio{3}}
A 20\,000-line failing program is useless to the person who must fix the compiler. \textbf{Delta debugging} (Zeller and Hildebrandt, 2002) shrinks an input automatically: split it into $n$ chunks, try removing each chunk (and its complement), keep any smaller input that still fails, otherwise double $n$ (smaller chunks). \texttt{ddmin} needs about $n^2$ test runs in the worst case and a few $\log n$ in the best; \textbf{hierarchical} delta debugging (Misherghi and Su, 2006) works on the syntax tree so that most candidates are valid; \textbf{C-Reduce} (Regehr et al., PLDI 2012; \texttt{cvise} is a Python port) adds C-aware passes (inline a function, remove a parameter, simplify an expression); \texttt{llvm-reduce} does the same for LLVM IR (functions, basic blocks, instructions, metadata, arguments\ldots{}). All of them need only an \textbf{interestingness test}: a script that exits 0 exactly when the candidate still shows \emph{the} bug.
\Needspace*{5\baselineskip}
\begin{lstlisting}[language={bash},basicstyle=\ttfamily\scriptsize,title={interesting.sh: the test that drives the reducer}]
#!/bin/bash
# exit 0  <=>  "interesting": the PTX generated for this IR still contains a vector store st.v4.u32
llc -O2 -march=nvptx64 -mcpu=sm_80 "$1" -o - 2>/dev/null | grep -q 'st\.v4\.u32'
\end{lstlisting}
\begin{lstlisting}[style=shcmd,basicstyle=\ttfamily\scriptsize]
$ chmod +x interesting.sh; llvm-stress -seed=1 -size=300 -o big.ll; llvm-reduce --test=./interesting.sh big.ll -o reduced.ll; wc -l big.ll reduced.ll; cat reduced.ll
\end{lstlisting}
\begin{lstlisting}[style=out,basicstyle=\ttfamily\scriptsize]
original is interesting
357 big.ll
9 reduced.ll
366 total
--- reduced.ll:

define void @autogen_SD1() {
BB:
  br label %CF239

CF239:                                            ; preds = %CF239, %BB
  store <4 x i32> zeroinitializer, ptr null, align 16
  br label %CF239
}
--- its PTX:
    st.v4.u32     [%rd1], {%r1, %r1, %r1, %r1};
\end{lstlisting}
357 lines became 9 (a vector store of zeros inside a loop), and the property is checked on the \emph{PTX}, not on the IR: that is how you would reduce ``the compiler emits this instruction (or crashes, or miscompiles)''. For a real miscompile the test is: compile at \texttt{-O0} and \texttt{-O3}, run both, outputs differ.
\begin{trapbox}[Reduction traps]
\textbf{(1) The reducer introduces undefined behaviour:} deleting an initialisation makes the miscompile ``reproduce'' because the program now reads garbage. Put the UB checks (\texttt{-Wall -Werror=uninitialized}, sanitizers, a warning-free build with a second compiler) \emph{inside} the interestingness test. \textbf{(2) A different bug:} the reduced program crashes with another message; compare the crash signature or the exact output difference, not just ``fails''. \textbf{(3) Non-determinism:} a flaky interestingness test makes the reducer wander; make it deterministic or run it several times. \textbf{(4) Slow tests:} give every command a timeout; most candidates are rejected, so the test must be cheap.
\end{trapbox}

\section{Isolating the culprit: bisection and triage \prio{2}}
\label{sec:bisect}
A reduced test case says \emph{what} fails; bisection says \emph{which change} (or pass, or flag) is responsible. Bisection is binary search on an ordered set, so 10\,000 commits need only $\lceil\log_2 10000\rceil=14$ test runs, provided the test is a deterministic script. \texttt{git bisect run} automates it: exit code \textbf{0 = good}, \textbf{1--127 (except 125) = bad}, \textbf{125 = cannot test this commit (skip)}, \textbf{$>$127 = abort}.
\Needspace*{5\baselineskip}
\begin{lstlisting}[language={bash},basicstyle=\ttfamily\scriptsize,title={bisect\_demo.sh: a 20-commit history, the bug arrives with commit 13}]
#!/bin/bash
set -e
rm -rf /tmp/bisect_repo && mkdir /tmp/bisect_repo && cd /tmp/bisect_repo
git init -q . && git config user.email t@example.com && git config user.name tester
cat > cc.sh <<'EOS'
#!/bin/bash
# toy "compiler": a / b with C semantics (truncation toward zero)
a=$1; b=$2
echo $(( a / b ))
EOS
cat > test.sh <<'EOS'
#!/bin/bash
[ "$(bash cc.sh -7 2)" = "-3" ]          # exit 0 = good build, 1 = bad build
EOS
for i in $(seq 1 20); do
  echo "# change $i" >> cc.sh
  if [ $i -eq 13 ]; then sed -i 's|echo $(( a / b ))|echo $(( (a - (a % b + b) % b) / b ))|' cc.sh; fi   # floor division sneaks in
  git add -A && git commit -q -m "commit $i$( [ $i -eq 13 ] && echo ': refactor division lowering')"
done
git bisect start HEAD "$(git rev-list --max-parents=0 HEAD)" >/dev/null
git bisect run bash test.sh > bisect.out 2>&1 || true
echo "history: 20 commits; test runs needed: $(grep -c running bisect.out) (log2 20 = 4.3)"
echo "first bad commit: $(git log -1 --format=%s refs/bisect/bad)"
\end{lstlisting}
\begin{lstlisting}[style=shcmd,basicstyle=\ttfamily\scriptsize]
$ ./bisect_demo.sh
\end{lstlisting}
\begin{lstlisting}[style=out,basicstyle=\ttfamily\scriptsize]
history: 20 commits; test runs needed: 5 (log2 20 = 4.3)
first bad commit: commit 13: refactor division lowering
\end{lstlisting}
\begin{center}\small
\begin{xltabular}{\textwidth}{@{}L{3.4cm}L{5.1cm}Y@{}}
\toprule
\textbf{Dimension} & \textbf{Tool} & \textbf{Note}\\
\midrule\endhead
commit / release & \texttt{git bisect run}; bisect between two nightly builds or between release branches & make the test a script with the exit-code protocol above; skip commits that do not build (125)\\
optimisation pass & LLVM: \texttt{-mllvm -opt-bisect-limit=N} (each transformation gets a number; binary-search $N$); \texttt{-print-after-all}, \texttt{-verify-each}; GCC: \texttt{-fno-<pass>}, \texttt{-fdbg-cnt=name:limit}, \texttt{-fdump-tree-all} & finds the pass that makes output wrong; then dump IR before/after it\\
function / translation unit & compile half the functions (or files) optimised, half not; \texttt{llvm-reduce} on the module & in a large build, bisect over object files\\
flags & remove options one by one (delta debugging on the command line) & \texttt{-O3 -march=\textbackslash{}ldots\{\} -ffast-math\textbackslash{}ldots} often hides a single culprit flag\\
target / architecture & same source for \texttt{sm\_70}, \texttt{sm\_80}, \texttt{sm\_90}; x86 vs AArch64 & separates generic from target-specific bugs\\
\bottomrule
\end{xltabular}
\end{center}
\textbf{Triage} turns a pile of failures into a short, ranked list of \emph{distinct} problems: (1) \textbf{classify} (crash, wrong code, hang, performance, diagnostic); (2) \textbf{deduplicate} by a normalised signature (assertion text plus the top stack frames with addresses and line numbers masked; for wrong code, the failing test area); (3) \textbf{rank} (silent wrong code $>$ crash on valid code $>$ rejects valid code $>$ performance regression $>$ diagnostics; weigh by how many users or tests are affected and whether it is a regression); (4) \textbf{route} to the owning component using the guilty pass or commit. Chapter~\ref{ch:lab} implements steps 1 and 2.

\section{Formal and semi-formal methods in one page \prio{2}}
\begin{itemize}[nosep]
\item \textbf{Translation validation} checks \emph{each compilation} instead of the compiler: prove that the output refines the input for this program. \textbf{Alive2} (Lopes, Lee, Hur, Liu, Regehr; PLDI 2021) does it for LLVM IR with an SMT solver; run over LLVM's own unit tests it found 47 new bugs (28 fixed at publication) and led to eight corrections of the LLVM Language Reference. It is \emph{bounded} (loops are unrolled up to a limit), so it can miss bugs, but everything it reports is real.
\item \textbf{A verified compiler} (CompCert; Leroy) comes with a machine-checked proof that compilation preserves semantics for the verified passes; Csmith found no wrong-code bugs in the verified passes, which is the strongest argument for proof; testing remains the way to cover what is not proved (parsers, assemblers, the hardware, the specification itself).
\item \textbf{Refinement vocabulary} to use correctly: \texttt{undef} and \texttt{poison} are the IR's ways of saying ``unspecified value'' and ``deferred undefined behaviour''; \texttt{nsw}/\texttt{nuw}/\texttt{inbounds} \emph{promise} something that, if false, yields poison; an optimisation is valid if every behaviour of the result is a behaviour of the source. Most real LLVM miscompilations are refinement errors: a pass keeps a flag it may not keep, or speculates something that may trap (Chapter~\ref{ch:llvm}).
\item \textbf{Limits:} proofs and checkers need a formal semantics of the source and target (for PTX and for the GPU memory model this is research-level work), cost time, and cannot cover the hardware.
\end{itemize}

\section{Test adequacy: coverage and mutation \prio{2}}
``How good is the test suite?'' has three practical answers. \textbf{Code coverage of the compiler} (\texttt{gcov}/\texttt{llvm-cov}, built with \texttt{-fprofile-instr-generate}) shows which passes and branches no test reaches. \textbf{Feature coverage} counts what the \emph{domain} offers: every PTX opcode $\times$ type $\times$ modifier (the matrix idea of Chapter~\ref{ch:ptx}), every \texttt{sm\_} target, every CUDA language feature. \textbf{Mutation testing} injects known faults into the compiler (change \texttt{<} to \texttt{<=}, delete a cache invalidation) and counts how many the test suite notices: the \emph{mutation score}. Surviving mutants say precisely which test is missing. The lab in Chapter~\ref{ch:lab} does this with eight seeded bugs and shows that a naive random generator kills only 5 of 8.


% ===== 17b-verif-plan.tex.in =====
\section{A test plan for a GPU and embedded compiler \prio{3}}
\label{sec:plan}
NVIDIA's internal infrastructure is not public; what follows is the standard layered structure used by industrial compiler teams (LLVM, GCC and vendor toolchains), adapted to PTX/CUDA and embedded targets. Use it as the skeleton for any ``design a verification flow for X'' question.
\begin{center}\small
\begin{xltabular}{\textwidth}{@{}L{2.9cm}L{4.5cm}L{4.1cm}Y@{}}
\toprule
\textbf{Layer} & \textbf{What is checked} & \textbf{Oracle / tools} & \textbf{Notes}\\
\midrule\endhead
\textbf{L0} unit and regression tests & one pass or lowering step on a tiny IR/PTX input & lit + FileCheck; IR and machine verifiers (\texttt{opt -verify-each}, \texttt{llc -verify-machineinstrs}) & milliseconds; every commit; tens of thousands of tests\\
\textbf{L1} instruction $\times$ type matrix & every PTX instruction $\times$ type $\times$ modifier $\times$ operand class $\times$ boundary value (0, $\pm1$, MIN/MAX, NaN, $\pm0$, denormals, rounding modes) & tests \emph{generated from a machine-readable ISA table}; CPU soft-float or \texttt{mpmath} reference; \texttt{ptxas} acceptance; SASS pattern checks & finds wrong encodings and semantics; scales with the ISA\\
\textbf{L2} language conformance & CUDA C++ and C++ features: templates, lambdas, \texttt{constexpr}, cooperative groups, atomics, \texttt{\_\_managed\_\_} & self-checking programs (return 0/1); expected-diagnostic tests for invalid code & one feature per test; compile-fail tests matter as much as run tests\\
\textbf{L3} random + differential & generated kernels and C programs (Section above) & \texttt{-O0} vs \texttt{-O3}; \texttt{sm\_XY} vs \texttt{sm\_ZW}; GPU vs CPU; release $N$ vs $N{-}1$; checksum; auto-reduction & nightly, millions of programs, seeds logged for replay\\
\textbf{L4} numerics & rounding modes, flush-to-zero, FMA contraction, fast-math flags, math-library accuracy, conversions (FP16, BF16, TF32, FP8) & ULP error against a high-precision reference; \emph{bit-exact} where IEEE-754 demands it (\texttt{+ - * / sqrt} with \texttt{.rn}) & results must match across architectures unless documented\\
\textbf{L5} memory model and concurrency & fences, scoped atomics, barrier semantics, divergence and reconvergence, race freedom & GPU \emph{litmus tests} run millions of times (Alglave et al., ASPLOS 2015, studied real GPUs); \texttt{compute-sanitizer}; checks that \texttt{convergent} operations are not moved & non-deterministic by nature: statistics, not single runs\\
\textbf{L6} real code & CUDA samples, library builds (BLAS/DNN-style), ML-compiler kernels (Triton), open-source applications, ``build the world'' & application self-checks, golden outputs with tolerances & finds integration and scale problems no unit test sees\\
\textbf{L7} performance and size & kernel time, SASS instruction count, registers, spills, occupancy, code size, compile time and memory & tracked per commit; thresholds with noise bands (median of $N$ runs); \texttt{-Xptxas -v} metrics & a 20\% slowdown is a bug even if the output is right\\
\textbf{L8} embedded / cross & cross-compilation (x86 host $\to$ AArch64 Linux or QNX target), ABI, alignment, \texttt{char} signedness, safety coding rules & board farm and simulators (QEMU-style); reproducible builds; MISRA/CERT static analysis; coverage evidence & qualification evidence (below)\\
\bottomrule
\end{xltabular}
\end{center}
\textbf{Infrastructure that makes the layers usable:} (1) \emph{scheduling and sharding} with a stable hash of the test name (\texttt{hash()} in Python is randomised per process; use \texttt{zlib.crc32}); (2) \emph{hermetic, pinned environments} (containers; compiler, driver and GPU model recorded with every result); (3) \emph{timeouts and resource limits} on every command; (4) \emph{retry and flake policy} (next section); (5) a \emph{result database} and dashboards: pass rate per release and per architecture, time-to-green; (6) \emph{automatic reduction, bisection and triage} so that a human sees one ranked list of distinct problems; (7) \emph{repro bundles}: source, flags, compiler version, GPU, driver, seed and log in one archive; (8) \emph{hardware farm management} (queues, GPU health checks, resets after hangs, priority tiers: pre-commit smoke tests, nightly full runs, weekly soaks).
\begin{srcbox}[Embedded and safety-related targets]
Automotive platforms are developed under ISO~26262, which treats a compiler as a \emph{software tool} (Part~8, ``confidence in the use of software tools''): you classify how likely the tool is to introduce or fail to detect an error and how likely you are to notice, obtain a \emph{tool confidence level}, and raise confidence by evidence: increased confidence from use, evaluation of the development process, or \emph{validation} --- a documented, systematic compiler test campaign. Practical consequences for a verification engineer: traceability from each requirement to its tests, coverage reports of compiler features, per-release known-bug lists, reproducible builds, and strict control of what changed between two releases. (Check the standard for exact wording; interviewers want the idea.)
\end{srcbox}

\section{Flaky tests and infrastructure hygiene \prio{2}}
A \textbf{flaky test} passes and fails on the same code and inputs. At scale it is the largest tax on a verification team: people stop trusting red results, and real regressions hide among false alarms.
\begin{center}\small
\begin{xltabular}{\textwidth}{@{}L{4.3cm}Y@{}}
\toprule
\textbf{Typical cause} & \textbf{Remedy}\\
\midrule\endhead
data races, timing assumptions, too-short timeouts & fix the test; synchronise instead of sleeping; calibrate timeouts from measured run times\\
shared state or order dependence (temp files, ports, environment) & private temp directories per test; unique ports; no reliance on test order\\
resource contention (CPU, disk, a GPU shared by parallel jobs) & limit parallelism per resource; pin tests to devices (\texttt{CUDA\_VISIBLE\_DEVICES}); queue GPU jobs\\
randomness without a logged seed & fixed or logged seeds; every failure must be replayable\\
hardware and infrastructure (thermal throttling, ECC errors, a dead node, licence or network servers) & health checks; classify \emph{infrastructure} failures separately from \emph{product} failures; auto-drain bad nodes\\
non-deterministic compiler output (hash-map iteration order, timestamps) & this is a product bug of its own (non-reproducible builds): fix it in the compiler\\
\bottomrule
\end{xltabular}
\end{center}
\textbf{Policy:} (1) \emph{never retry silently}: a test that fails then passes is reported as \textbf{FLAKY} and counted (Chapter~\ref{ch:lab}'s \texttt{litlite} does this); (2) track a flake rate per test and alert when it rises; (3) \textbf{quarantine} with an owner and an expiry date --- a skipped test is lost coverage; (4) re-run a failing test $k$ times to separate always-failing (real) from sometimes-failing (flaky); (5) timeouts are results, not noise: a hang is a bug until proven otherwise; (6) store seed, command line and environment with every failure.

\section{Writing the bug report \prio{2}}
\label{sec:report}
\begin{defbox}[A report a compiler engineer will thank you for]
\begin{description}[nosep,leftmargin=2.7cm,style=nextline]
\item[Title] \texttt{[component] symptom}, e.g.\ ``\texttt{[NVPTX] wrong result: signed division by constant folds with floor semantics}''.
\item[Environment] compiler version or commit, target (\texttt{sm\_80}), host OS, driver, \emph{exact} command line.
\item[Reproducer] the \emph{reduced}, UB-free source (sanitizer-clean, warning-free), and the commands to build and run it.
\item[Expected / actual] with the justification (spec section, result from another compiler or from the interpreter) and the verbatim output, crash message or diff.
\item[Analysis] bisection result (commit or pass), IR or PTX before and after the guilty pass, what you already ruled out (UB, flags).
\item[Impact] always or intermittent; regression since which release; which code is affected; workaround (flag or source change).
\end{description}
One bug per report; text as text (no screenshots); attach files; do not guess at the root cause in the title.
\end{defbox}

\section{Practice questions \prio{3}}
\begin{qn}How would you test a compiler?\end{qn}
\begin{ans}Layers (Section~\ref{sec:plan}): \emph{(1)} fast regression tests with lit/FileCheck for each pass; \emph{(2)} conformance tests for the language and, for GPUs, a generated instruction$\times$type$\times$modifier matrix against a CPU reference; \emph{(3)} random UB-free programs with \emph{differential} testing across optimisation levels, targets, compilers and releases, and \emph{metamorphic} variants where no reference exists; \emph{(4)} automatic reduction of every failure and bisection to the commit or pass; \emph{(5)} numerics, memory-model and performance suites; \emph{(6)} real applications; \emph{(7)} all of it in sharded CI with timeouts, flake handling, triage by signature, and coverage/mutation measurements of the suite itself.\end{ans}
\begin{qn}What is a test oracle? Name four that are usable for a compiler.\end{qn}
\begin{ans}The mechanism that decides whether an observed behaviour is correct. Golden expected output (FileCheck, self-checking programs); a second implementation (differential); a behaviour-preserving transformation of the input (metamorphic/EMI); a reference model (interpreter, CPU for GPU code, high-precision maths library); invariants (verifiers, sanitizers, idempotence, determinism); proofs (translation validation).\end{ans}
\begin{qn}Differential versus metamorphic testing: strengths, weaknesses, one example each.\end{qn}
\begin{ans}Differential needs two implementations (e.g.\ \texttt{-O0} vs \texttt{-O3}) and finds any bug on which they disagree, but not bugs shared by both; it needs defined-behaviour inputs. Metamorphic needs only one implementation and a transformation with a known effect (insert code that is dead for the input, replace \texttt{e} by \texttt{e+0}), so it works where there is no reference, but it only sees bugs that treat the original and the variant differently. In the lab (Chapter~\ref{ch:lab}) two seeded bugs were found by differential but never by metamorphic testing for exactly that reason.\end{ans}
\begin{qn}A program prints different results at \texttt{-O0} and \texttt{-O2}. Is it a compiler bug?\end{qn}
\begin{ans}Usually not. Check determinism; run UBSan/ASan/MSan/valgrind/TSan and \texttt{-Wall -Wextra}; look for unspecified or implementation-defined behaviour (evaluation order, \texttt{char} signedness, FP contraction), strict-aliasing and lifetime violations. Only if the program is clean, localise (pass bisection, IR diff), reduce, re-check the reduced program, and report. The table of six programs earlier in this chapter shows six disagreements and zero compiler bugs.\end{ans}
\begin{qn}Why must generated programs avoid undefined behaviour, and how do Csmith or YARPGen manage it?\end{qn}
\begin{ans}With UB the compiler may legally do anything, so a mismatch proves nothing and testing drowns in false positives. Generators avoid UB by construction: initialised variables, wrapped or guarded arithmetic (safe-math helpers, unsigned wrap-around, masked shifts), bounded loops, valid indices, static analysis of pointers (Csmith), and tracking value ranges during generation so that no operation can overflow (YARPGen, without run-time checks).\end{ans}
\begin{qn}Explain delta debugging and the interestingness test; name two pitfalls.\end{qn}
\begin{ans}Repeatedly remove chunks of the input (halves, quarters, \ldots) and keep any smaller input for which a test script still reports the failure; stop when no single removal works (1-minimal). Worst case $\sim n^2$ test runs. Pitfalls: the reduced program drifts into undefined behaviour or into a different bug (so check the \emph{same} symptom and run sanitizers inside the test), and flaky or slow tests (give timeouts, make them deterministic). Hierarchical variants and C-Reduce/\texttt{llvm-reduce} use the syntax so that most candidates are valid.\end{ans}
\begin{qn}Between a good and a bad build lie 10\,000 commits. How many builds do you need, and what if some commits do not build?\end{qn}
\begin{ans}$\lceil\log_2 10\,000\rceil=14$ test runs by binary search. Commits that do not build return exit code 125 (``skip'') from the \texttt{git bisect run} script; git then picks a neighbour. A non-monotonic history (the bug appears, disappears and reappears) can mislead bisection: make sure the test checks \emph{one} bug.\end{ans}
\begin{qn}What is a flaky test and how should the infrastructure treat it?\end{qn}
\begin{ans}One that both passes and fails on identical code. Log retries and report FLAKY instead of hiding it; keep a per-test flake rate; quarantine with owner and expiry rather than silently skipping; classify infrastructure versus product failures; store seeds and environment so a failure is replayable; treat timeouts as results.\end{ans}
\begin{qn}A new instruction \texttt{foo.bf16x2} was added to the compiler. How do you verify it?\end{qn}
\begin{ans}Build the test matrix from the ISA description: every type and modifier combination (rounding, saturation, flush-to-zero), operand classes (register, immediate), and \emph{boundary values} (0, $\pm1$, max/min, infinities, NaNs with payloads, denormals). Compute expected results with a bit-exact CPU reference (soft float or a high-precision library). Check that \texttt{ptxas} accepts the PTX and that the SASS is plausible (FileCheck), run on every supported \texttt{sm\_} target, compare across optimisation levels, include the instruction in random-kernel generation, and add regression tests for each bug found.\end{ans}
\begin{qn}Translation validation versus a verified compiler?\end{qn}
\begin{ans}A verified compiler (CompCert) carries a machine-checked proof for the compiler itself: all future compilations are correct. Translation validation checks each \emph{individual} compilation (source vs.\ output, usually with an SMT solver, e.g.\ Alive2 for LLVM IR): no proof of the compiler is needed, and it can be applied to an existing, unverified compiler, but it checks only what it is run on and is typically bounded.\end{ans}
\begin{qn}Design a random generator for CUDA kernels whose results are deterministic.\end{qn}
\begin{ans}One output slot per thread (no races); expression DAGs over integers with safe-math wrappers; bounded loops; initialised memory; barriers only in block-uniform control flow, shared-memory phases as write--barrier--read; divergence from \texttt{threadIdx}- and data-dependent conditions; no floating-point atomics (or tolerance-based comparison); print a checksum per kernel. Oracle: the same source compiled for the CPU (as in Chapter~\ref{ch:cuda}), plus differential comparison across optimisation levels, \texttt{sm\_} targets and releases; reduce failures automatically.\end{ans}
\begin{qn}The compiler crashes and prints ``Running pass \texttt{X} on function \texttt{Y}''. First steps?\end{qn}
\begin{ans}Save the exact command line and compiler version; re-run to confirm determinism; reduce the input (\texttt{llvm-reduce} with the crash message as interestingness test); get the IR just before the pass (\texttt{-mllvm -print-before=X}, or \texttt{-mllvm -opt-bisect-limit}, \texttt{llc -stop-before=X}); run with assertions or verifiers enabled (\texttt{-verify-each}, \texttt{-verify-machineinstrs}); check for a duplicate in the tracker by crash signature; file with the reduced input.\end{ans}
\begin{qn}How do you know your test suite is any good?\end{qn}
\begin{ans}Coverage of the compiler (which passes and branches no test reaches), feature/matrix coverage (instructions $\times$ types $\times$ targets), and \emph{mutation testing}: inject known bugs and measure the fraction the suite catches; every surviving mutant names a missing test. Also: bug-escape analysis (for each bug found later by users, why did no test catch it?).\end{ans}
\begin{qn}The same CUDA kernel gives slightly different floating-point results on two GPU generations. Bug?\end{qn}
\begin{ans}Not necessarily: fused multiply-add contraction (\texttt{--fmad}), different math-library implementations (documented ULP error bounds), flush-to-zero defaults, and different reduction orders all change the last bits. Compare with a tolerance derived from the documented bounds; for bit-exact IEEE operations (\texttt{+ - * /}, \texttt{sqrt} with round-to-nearest) results \emph{must} match, so a difference there is a bug. Re-run with \texttt{--fmad=false -ftz=false -prec-div=true -prec-sqrt=true} to separate contraction effects from real defects.\end{ans}


% ===== 18a-lab-build.tex.in =====
\chapter{The Automation Lab: A Mini Verification Framework in Python}
\label{ch:lab}

\begin{chapbox}
\textbf{Priority \prio{3} for the ideas, \prio{2} for the code. Time: 120 min to read and run everything; 45 min if you only read the results.} The job asks you to ``design and implement tools, libraries, and automation to support compiler development and verification'' and lists Python as a core skill. This chapter builds, from scratch and in about 500 lines, the workflow of Chapter~\ref{ch:verif}: a random program generator, a differential tester, metamorphic variants, an automatic test-case reducer, seeded bugs to \emph{measure} the whole thing (mutation testing), a mini \texttt{FileCheck} and a mini \texttt{lit} with timeouts, retries, flake detection and sharding, and a triage report. The system under test is the \textbf{Tiny compiler} of Chapter~\ref{ch:sem} (source $\to$ three-address code $\to$ optimiser $\to$ virtual machine; listing in Appendix~\ref{app:tiny}). Every listing is the \emph{actual file} the build wrote and ran; every number is captured output of that run, with fixed seeds so you can reproduce it.
\end{chapbox}

\section{What we build \prio{3}}
\begin{center}
\begin{tikzpicture}[node distance=5mm and 6mm,every node/.style={font=\scriptsize},
 box/.style={draw=nvdark,fill=nvgreen!12,rounded corners=2pt,minimum height=8mm,align=center,text width=2.35cm},
 sut/.style={draw=black!60,fill=black!5,rounded corners=2pt,minimum height=8mm,align=center,text width=2.35cm},
 arr/.style={-{Stealth[length=2mm]},thick,nvdark}]
\node[box] (gen) {\texttt{tinygen.py}\\ random programs};
\node[sut,right=of gen] (sut) {compiler under test: \texttt{tiny.py} or a mutant};
\node[sut,above=of sut] (ref) {oracle: reference interpreter};
\node[box,right=of sut] (cmp) {\texttt{difftest.py}\\ compare outputs};
\node[box,right=of cmp] (red) {\texttt{tinyreduce.py}\\ minimal reproducer};
\node[box,below=8mm of red] (reg) {\texttt{labtests.py}\\ lit regression test};
\node[box,left=of reg] (lit) {\texttt{litlite.py}, \texttt{filecheck\_lite.py}};
\node[box,left=of lit] (rep) {\texttt{report.py}\\ triage report};
\draw[arr] (gen)--(sut); \draw[arr] (gen.north) |- (ref.west); \draw[arr] (sut)--(cmp); \draw[arr] (ref.east) -| (cmp.north);
\draw[arr] (cmp)--(red); \draw[arr] (red)--(reg); \draw[arr] (reg)--(lit); \draw[arr] (lit)--(rep);
\end{tikzpicture}
\end{center}
\begin{center}\small
\begin{xltabular}{\textwidth}{@{}L{3.6cm}L{4.3cm}Y@{}}
\toprule
\textbf{Lab file} & \textbf{What it does} & \textbf{Industry counterpart}\\
\midrule\endhead
\texttt{tinygen.py} & random, always-terminating, UB-free programs; generation \emph{policies}; metamorphic variants & Csmith, YARPGen, CLsmith, GraphicsFuzz\\
\texttt{difftest.py} & compile and run, compare with the reference interpreter; classify pass / miscompile / ICE & cross-compiler, cross-\texttt{-O}, cross-target harnesses\\
\texttt{mutants.py}, \texttt{study.py} & eight seeded compiler bugs; which testing method catches which; reproducers & mutation testing; fault-injection studies\\
\texttt{tinyreduce.py} & hierarchical delta debugging on the syntax tree & C-Reduce, \texttt{cvise}, \texttt{llvm-reduce}\\
\texttt{labtests.py}, \texttt{tinyc.py} & turn a reproducer into a lit test with golden output & the ``add a regression test'' step of every bug fix\\
\texttt{filecheck\_lite.py} & \texttt{CHECK}, \texttt{CHECK-NEXT}, \texttt{CHECK-NOT}, \texttt{\{\{regex\}\}} & LLVM \texttt{FileCheck} (cross-validated against it below)\\
\texttt{litlite.py} & discover tests, run \texttt{RUN:} lines in parallel; timeout, retry, XFAIL, shard, JSON & LLVM \texttt{lit}\\
\texttt{report.py} & summary table and failure grouping by normalised signature & nightly dashboards, triage bots\\
\bottomrule
\end{xltabular}
\end{center}
\textbf{The language.} Tiny has integer variables (unbounded), arithmetic with C-style truncating division, comparisons, \texttt{\&\&}, \texttt{||}, \texttt{!}, assignment, \texttt{if}/\texttt{else}, \texttt{while} and \texttt{print}; division by zero is a defined \emph{trap} (so there is no undefined behaviour to avoid, only traps to preserve). The optimiser does constant folding and propagation, copy propagation, algebraic simplification, common-subexpression elimination, dead-code elimination, branch and jump clean-up, and temporary coalescing; it was fuzzed against the interpreter before this chapter existed, so \emph{the pristine compiler is expected to pass everything}.

\section{Step 1: a random program generator \prio{3}}
Design rules (the same as for Csmith, in miniature): \textbf{every program terminates} (each loop has its own counter variable per nesting level, which the body never assigns); \textbf{no variable is read before it is set}; \textbf{divisors are usually $\ge1$} (\texttt{rhs*rhs+1}), but one in ten is a raw expression that may trap; \textbf{variables \texttt{c}--\texttt{f} are scratch} (only \texttt{a} and \texttt{b} are printed at the end), so dead code arises naturally; everything is driven by one seed. Three \textbf{policies} change \emph{what} is generated, not how valid it is: \texttt{plain}; \texttt{reuse} (re-use small earlier expressions, sometimes with swapped operands, to feed common-subexpression elimination); \texttt{full} (\texttt{reuse} plus small \emph{idioms} that optimisers like to rewrite: copy-then-clobber, a dead division, \texttt{x-y} next to \texttt{y-x}). A separate random stream applies \textbf{meaning-preserving edits} when \texttt{meta=True} (used in Step 4): value-preserving wrappers (\texttt{e+0}, \texttt{1*e}, \texttt{-(-e)}, \texttt{e-0}) and never-taken branches.
\Needspace*{5\baselineskip}
\begin{lstlisting}[language={Python},basicstyle=\ttfamily\fontsize{7.2}{8.6}\selectfont,title={tinygen.py: the generator}]
"""tinygen.py -- random generator of well-defined Tiny programs.
Every loop terminates and no variable is read before it is set."""
import random

VARS = ["a", "b", "c", "d", "e", "f"]      # a and b are printed at the end; c..f are scratch (can become dead)
CONSTS = [0, 1, 2, 3, 5, 7, 10, 100]
OPS = ["+", "-", "*", "/", "%", "<", "<=", ">", ">=", "==", "!=", "&&", "||"]

def nodes(e):                                # number of AST nodes of an expression tuple
    return 1 + sum(nodes(x) for x in e[2:]) if e[0] in ("bin", "un") else 1

def show(e, m=None):                         # expression tuple -> source; an RNG m adds identity rewrites
    k = e[0]
    if k == "num": s = str(e[1]) if e[1] >= 0 else "(-%d)" % -e[1]
    elif k == "var": s = e[1]
    elif k == "un": s = "(%s%s)" % (e[1], show(e[2], m))
    else: s = "(%s %s %s)" % (show(e[2], m), e[1], show(e[3], m))
    if m is not None and k in ("bin", "un") and m.random() < 0.15:      # metamorphic edit: value-preserving wrapper
        s = m.choice(["(%s + 0)", "(1 * %s)", "(%s - 0)", "(-(-%s))"]) % s
    return s

class Gen:
    def __init__(self, seed, policy="full", meta=False):    # policy: "plain" | "reuse" | "full" (reuse + idioms)
        self.r, self.policy, self.recent, self.meta = random.Random(seed), policy, [], meta
        self.m = random.Random(seed * 7919 + 1)             # separate stream: edits never disturb the base program

    def sh(self, e):
        return show(e, self.m if self.meta else None)

    def dead_branch(self):                                  # a branch that is never taken: v*v + 1 >= 1
        v, w = self.m.choice(VARS), self.m.choice(VARS)
        cond = show(("bin", "==", ("bin", "+", ("bin", "*", ("var", v), ("var", v)), ("num", 1)), ("num", 0)))
        return "if (%s) { %s = %s + 1; print(%s); } " % (cond, w, v, w)

    def expr(self, depth):
        r = self.r
        small = [x for x in self.recent if nodes(x) <= 7]   # (keeps programs from growing exponentially)
        if self.policy != "plain" and small and r.random() < 0.25:     # policy: re-use an earlier small expression
            e = r.choice(small)
            if e[1] in ("+", "-", "*") and r.random() < 0.5:           # ... sometimes with swapped operands
                e = ("bin", e[1], e[3], e[2])
            return e
        if depth == 0 or r.random() < 0.25:
            if r.random() < 0.5: return ("num", r.choice(CONSTS) * r.choice([1, 1, -1]))
            return ("var", r.choice(VARS))
        x = r.random()
        if x < 0.06: return ("un", "-", self.expr(depth - 1))
        if x < 0.10: return ("un", "!", self.expr(depth - 1))
        op, lhs, rhs = r.choice(OPS), self.expr(depth - 1), self.expr(depth - 1)
        if op in ("/", "%") and r.random() < 0.9:           # usually keep the divisor >= 1: rhs*rhs + 1
            rhs = ("bin", "+", ("bin", "*", rhs, rhs), ("num", 1))
        e = ("bin", op, lhs, rhs)
        self.recent = (self.recent + [e])[-6:]
        return e

    def idiom(self):                                        # policy: small patterns that optimisers like to rewrite
        r, sh = self.r, self.sh
        v, w = r.sample(VARS, 2)
        k = r.randint(0, 2)
        if k == 0:                                          # copy, then clobber the source
            return "%s = %s; %s = %s; print(%s);" % (v, w, w, sh(self.expr(2)), v)
        if k == 1:                                          # a dead division that may trap
            return "z = %s / %s;" % (sh(self.expr(2)), sh(self.expr(2)))
        vv, ww = ("var", v), ("var", w)                     # x-y next to y-x
        return "%s = %s; %s = %s;" % (v, sh(("bin", "-", vv, ww)), w, sh(("bin", "-", ww, vv)))

    def stmt(self, depth, level):
        text = self.stmt0(depth, level)
        return self.dead_branch() + text if self.meta and self.m.random() < 0.15 else text

    def stmt0(self, depth, level):
        r, x = self.r, self.r.random()
        if self.policy == "full" and r.random() < 0.12: return self.idiom()
        if depth == 0 or x < 0.55:
            v = r.choice(VARS)
            if r.random() < 0.2: return "%s = %s;" % (v, r.choice(VARS))       # plain copy: feeds copy propagation
            return "%s = %s;" % (v, self.sh(self.expr(3)))
        if x < 0.65: return "print(%s);" % self.sh(self.expr(3))
        if x < 0.85:
            s = "if (%s) { %s }" % (self.sh(self.expr(2)), self.block(depth - 1, level))
            return s + (" else { %s }" % self.block(depth - 1, level) if r.random() < 0.5 else "")
        i, n = "i%d" % level, r.randint(1, 4)               # one loop counter per nesting level: loops terminate
        body = self.block(depth - 1, level + 1)
        return "%s = 0; while (%s < %d) { %s %s = %s + 1; }" % (i, i, n, body, i, i)

    def block(self, depth, level):
        return " ".join(self.stmt(depth, level) for _ in range(self.r.randint(1, 3)))

def program(seed, stmts=10, policy="full", meta=False):
    g = Gen(seed, policy, meta)
    init = " ".join("%s = %d;" % (v, g.r.choice(CONSTS)) for v in VARS)
    return init + "\n" + "\n".join(g.stmt(2, 0) for _ in range(stmts)) + "\nprint(a); print(b);\n"
\end{lstlisting}
\begin{lstlisting}[language={Python},basicstyle=\ttfamily\scriptsize]
import statistics, tiny
from tinygen import program
srcs = [program(s) for s in range(300)]
asts = [tiny.parse(x) for x in srcs]                          # every generated program must parse ...
res = [tiny.interpret(a) for a in asts]                       # ... and run to completion or to a defined trap
print("300 programs: parse errors 0 (a parse error would have raised); mean length %d characters (max %d)"
      % (statistics.mean(len(x) for x in srcs), max(len(x) for x in srcs)))
print("end in a trap: %d | hit the step limit: %d | mean number of printed values: %.1f"
      % (sum(t == "division by zero" for _, t in res), sum(t == "step limit" for _, t in res), statistics.mean(len(o) for o, _ in res)))
print("with a loop: %d | with if/else: %d | with a division or %%: %d"
      % (sum("while" in x for x in srcs), sum("else" in x for x in srcs), sum(("/" in x) or ("%" in x) for x in srcs)))
print("\n--- program(7), first lines:")
for line in program(7).split("\n")[:5]: print(line[:110] + (" ..." if len(line) > 110 else ""))
\end{lstlisting}

\section{Step 2: differential testing against a reference interpreter \prio{3}}
The oracle is the \emph{tree-walking interpreter of the pristine compiler}: a second, independent implementation of the language (it shares only the arithmetic helper with the virtual machine). The system under test compiles the program to three-address code, optimises it and runs it on the virtual machine. Outcomes: \textbf{pass}, \textbf{MISCOMPILE} (different output or trap behaviour), \textbf{ICE} (the compiler raised an exception), \textbf{skip} (step limit reached on either side: inconclusive, not a failure). Note the design choice that matters most later: the oracle always comes from the \emph{pristine} module even when the system under test is a mutant; otherwise a bug in shared code would affect both sides and cancel out (a common-mode failure).
\Needspace*{5\baselineskip}
\begin{lstlisting}[language={Python},basicstyle=\ttfamily\fontsize{7.2}{8.6}\selectfont,title={difftest.py: oracle, classification, campaign}]
"""difftest.py -- differential and metamorphic testing of a Tiny compiler."""
import tiny as golden                 # the pristine compiler: its tree-walking interpreter is the oracle
from tinygen import program

def check(src, compiler, opt=True):
    ref = golden.interpret(golden.parse(src))          # (output list, trap message or None)
    try:
        out, trap, _ = compiler.run_tac(compiler.compile_source(src, opt))
    except Exception as e:                              # an exception inside the compiler is an ICE
        return "ICE", "%s: %s" % (type(e).__name__, e)
    if "step limit" in (ref[1], trap): return "skip", None
    return ("pass", None) if (ref[0], ref[1]) == (out, trap) else ("MISCOMPILE", (ref, (out, trap)))

def campaign(compiler, n=300, first=0, opt=True):
    """Run n random programs; return {'pass': k, ...} and the list of (seed, kind, detail) failures."""
    counts, failures = {}, []
    for seed in range(first, first + n):
        kind, detail = check(program(seed), compiler, opt)
        counts[kind] = counts.get(kind, 0) + 1
        if kind in ("MISCOMPILE", "ICE"): failures.append((seed, kind, detail))
    return counts, failures

def run_sut(src, compiler, opt=True):
    out, trap, _ = compiler.run_tac(compiler.compile_source(src, opt))
    return out, trap

def meta_check(seed, compiler):
    """Oracle-free (metamorphic): a program and its meaning-preserving variant must
    behave identically under the SAME compiler."""
    try:
        a, b = run_sut(program(seed), compiler), run_sut(program(seed, meta=True), compiler)
    except Exception as e:
        return "ICE", "%s: %s" % (type(e).__name__, e)
    if "step limit" in (a[1], b[1]): return "skip", None
    return ("pass", None) if a == b else ("MISCOMPILE", (a, b))
\end{lstlisting}
\begin{lstlisting}[language={Python},basicstyle=\ttfamily\scriptsize]
import difftest, tiny
print("pristine compiler vs reference interpreter, 300 random programs")
for opt in (False, True):
    counts, failures = difftest.campaign(tiny, 300, opt=opt)
    print("  optimiser %-3s: %s" % ("on" if opt else "off", counts))
\end{lstlisting}
\Needspace*{5\baselineskip}
\begin{lstlisting}[style=out,basicstyle=\ttfamily\scriptsize,title={Captured output}]
pristine compiler vs reference interpreter, 300 random programs
  optimiser off: {'pass': 300}
  optimiser on : {'pass': 300}
\end{lstlisting}
All 300 programs agree with and without the optimiser: the baseline is clean, so any later disagreement is a seeded bug. (A clean baseline is itself a result: it took one earlier round of fuzzing, three bugs in the optimiser and a rewrite of its jump clean-up to get here.)

\section{Step 3: seeded bugs, so that the method can be measured \prio{3}}
How do you know a random tester is any good? Break the compiler on purpose and see whether the tester notices (\textbf{mutation testing}, or fault injection). Each entry below rewrites one spot of \texttt{tiny.py} and loads the result as a separate module; the table of what they model is under the listing.
\Needspace*{5\baselineskip}
\begin{lstlisting}[language={Python},basicstyle=\ttfamily\fontsize{7.2}{8.6}\selectfont,title={mutants.py: eight seeded bugs}]
"""mutants.py -- seeded compiler bugs: each entry rewrites one spot of tiny.py.
Every mutant is loaded as its own module, so the pristine compiler stays available as the oracle."""
import os, re, types
SRC = open(os.path.join(os.path.dirname(os.path.abspath(__file__)), "tiny.py")).read()

MUTANTS = {                                    # name: (regex of the original text, replacement)
  # / is folded with floor instead of truncation toward zero
  "fold-floor-div": (r'BUGGY_FOLD = False', 'BUGGY_FOLD = True'),
  # x - 0  ==>  0
  "sub-zero": (r'elif op == "-" and b == 0: res = a',
               'elif op == "-" and b == 0: res = 0'),
  # value numbering treats a-b and b-a as the same expression
  "cse-commutes-all": (r'key = \(op, a, b\) if op not in "\+\*" else \(op,\) \+ tuple\(sorted\(\(a, b\), key=str\)\)',
                       'key = (op,) + tuple(sorted((a, b), key=str))'),
  # off-by-one when a branch is inverted: "<" becomes ">" instead of ">="
  "invert-lt": (r'INVERT = \{"<": ">=",', 'INVERT = {"<": ">",'),
  # copy facts are not invalidated when their source is redefined
  "stale-copy": (r' *for k in \[k for k, v in env.items\(\) if v == name\]: del env\[k\]\n', ''),
  # dead-code elimination also deletes divisions that could trap
  "dce-trap": (r'used and\n\s+not \(ins\[0\] == "bin".*?\)\)\)\]', 'used)]'),
  # index error in a helper: the compiler crashes (ICE) on valid input
  "uses-crash": (r'elif k == "un": xs = \(ins\[3\],\)', 'elif k == "un": xs = (ins[4],)'),
  # coalesces temporaries that are used more than once
  "coalesce-multi": (r'count.get\(ins\[1\]\) == 1', 'count.get(ins[1], 0) >= 1'),
}

def load(name):
    pattern, repl = MUTANTS[name]
    text, n = re.subn(pattern, lambda m: repl, SRC, flags=re.S)
    assert n == 1, "mutation site for %s must match exactly once (matched %d)" % (name, n)
    mod = types.ModuleType("tiny_" + name.replace("-", "_"))
    exec(compile(text, "<mutant %s>" % name, "exec"), mod.__dict__)
    return mod
\end{lstlisting}
\begin{center}\small
\begin{xltabular}{\textwidth}{@{}L{3.4cm}L{5.0cm}Y@{}}
\toprule
\textbf{Mutant} & \textbf{The bug} & \textbf{Real-world family}\\
\midrule\endhead
\texttt{fold-floor-div} & constant-folds \texttt{/} with floor instead of truncation toward zero & wrong constant folding (rounding, \texttt{INT\_MIN / -1}, shifts)\\
\texttt{sub-zero} & simplifies \texttt{x - 0} to \texttt{0} & wrong algebraic identity\\
\texttt{cse-commutes-all} & value numbering treats \texttt{a-b} and \texttt{b-a} as the same expression & non-commutative operator handled as commutative\\
\texttt{invert-lt} & inverts \texttt{<} into \texttt{>} instead of \texttt{>=} when a branch is flipped & off-by-one at a boundary in branch folding\\
\texttt{stale-copy} & copy facts are not invalidated when the source is redefined & stale data-flow fact\\
\texttt{dce-trap} & dead-code elimination deletes a division that could trap & removing an instruction with an observable effect\\
\texttt{uses-crash} & index error in a helper function & ICE: crash on valid input\\
\texttt{coalesce-multi} & merges a temporary that is used more than once & unsound optimisation precondition\\
\bottomrule
\end{xltabular}
\end{center}

\section{Step 4: metamorphic variants (no reference needed) \prio{2}}
\label{sec:meta}
\texttt{meta\_check} in \texttt{difftest.py} compiles the program and a variant that differs only by value-preserving wrappers and never-taken branches; the \emph{same} compiler must treat both identically. The variant is built by the generator's second random stream, so the base program is unchanged. First check that the transformations really preserve meaning, and look at one variant:
\begin{lstlisting}[language={Python},basicstyle=\ttfamily\scriptsize]
import difflib, tiny
from tinygen import program
bad = sum(tiny.interpret(tiny.parse(program(s))) != tiny.interpret(tiny.parse(program(s, meta=True))) for s in range(300))
print("300 seeds: interpreter disagrees between a program and its metamorphic variant in %d cases" % bad)
print("\n--- first differences, program(3) vs program(3, meta=True) (cut to 100 columns):")
a, b = program(3).split("\n"), program(3, meta=True).split("\n")
for l in list(difflib.unified_diff(a, b, lineterm="", n=0))[2:8]: print(l[:100])
\end{lstlisting}

-c = (((a * 100) && (e % ((e * e) + 1))) && (-(e && b)));

+c = ((((a * 100) && (e % ((e * e) + 1))) + 0) && (-(e && b)));
