\section{Step 5: automatic test-case reduction \prio{3}}
A failing random program has hundreds of tokens; the person who fixes the bug wants five. \texttt{tinyreduce.py} implements the idea of C-Reduce in miniature: \textbf{greedy hierarchical reduction} on the syntax tree. \texttt{shrinks} yields simpler variants of a node, most aggressive first: delete chunks of statements (halves, quarters, single statements: the ddmin idea), splice a nested block into its parent, keep only the \texttt{then}- or \texttt{else}-part of an \texttt{if}, run a loop body once, replace an expression by one of its operands or by a constant, shrink literals. \texttt{reduce} accepts the first smaller candidate that is still \emph{interesting} and starts again, until nothing helps (a local minimum, ``1-minimal'' for these transformations). \texttt{same\_failure} is the interestingness test: it demands the \emph{same kind} of failure (for crashes, the same exception text), because reducing towards a different bug is the classic trap.
\Needspace*{5\baselineskip}
\begin{lstlisting}[language={Python},basicstyle=\ttfamily\fontsize{7.2}{8.6}\selectfont,title={tinyreduce.py: hierarchical delta debugging}]
"""tinyreduce.py -- hierarchical delta debugging for Tiny programs: a miniature C-Reduce."""
import tiny, difftest

def unparse(n):                                   # AST -> source text
    k = n[0]
    if k == "num":   return str(n[1]) if n[1] >= 0 else "(-%d)" % -n[1]
    if k == "var":   return n[1]
    if k == "un":    return "(%s%s)" % (n[1], unparse(n[2]))
    if k == "bin":   return "(%s %s %s)" % (unparse(n[2]), n[1], unparse(n[3]))
    if k == "assign": return "%s = %s;" % (n[1], unparse(n[2]))
    if k == "print": return "print(%s);" % unparse(n[1])
    if k == "block": return "{ " + " ".join(unparse(s) for s in n[1]) + " }"
    if k == "if":    return "if (%s) %s" % (unparse(n[1]), unparse(n[2])) + (" else " + unparse(n[3]) if n[3] else "")
    if k == "while": return "while (%s) %s" % (unparse(n[1]), unparse(n[2]))

def source(ast): return "\n".join(unparse(s) for s in ast[1])

def size(n):                              # smaller = simpler; a literal costs its bit length (100 -> 0 is progress)
    if isinstance(n, tuple): return 1 + sum(size(x) for x in n[1:])
    if isinstance(n, list):  return sum(size(x) for x in n)
    return n.bit_length() if isinstance(n, int) and n > 0 else 0

def shrinks(n):                                   # candidate simplifications of a node, most aggressive first
    k = n[0]
    if k == "block":
        ss, step = n[1], max(len(n[1]) // 2, 1)
        while ss:                                 # 1. delete chunks of statements: halves, quarters, ..., singles
            for i in range(0, len(ss), step):
                yield ("block", ss[:i] + ss[i + step:])
            if step == 1: break
            step //= 2
        for i, s in enumerate(ss):                # 2. splice a nested block into its parent
            if s[0] == "block": yield ("block", ss[:i] + s[1] + ss[i + 1:])
        for i, s in enumerate(ss):                # 3. simplify one statement in place
            for s2 in shrinks(s): yield ("block", ss[:i] + [s2] + ss[i + 1:])
    elif k == "if":
        yield n[2]                                # keep only the then-part ...
        if n[3] is not None: yield n[3]; yield ("if", n[1], n[2], None)     # ... or the else-part, or drop the else
        for c in shrinks(n[1]): yield ("if", c, n[2], n[3])
        for t in shrinks(n[2]): yield ("if", n[1], t, n[3])
        if n[3] is not None:
            for e in shrinks(n[3]): yield ("if", n[1], n[2], e)
    elif k == "while":
        yield n[2]                                # run the body once, drop the loop
        for c in shrinks(n[1]): yield ("while", c, n[2])
        for b in shrinks(n[2]): yield ("while", n[1], b)
    elif k in ("assign", "print"):
        i = 2 if k == "assign" else 1
        for e in shrinks(n[i]): yield n[:i] + (e,) + n[i + 1:]
    elif k == "bin":
        yield n[2]; yield n[3]; yield ("num", 0); yield ("num", 1)         # replace by an operand or a constant
        for l in shrinks(n[2]): yield ("bin", n[1], l, n[3])
        for r in shrinks(n[3]): yield ("bin", n[1], n[2], r)
    elif k == "un":
        yield n[2]; yield ("num", 0)
        for e in shrinks(n[2]): yield ("un", n[1], e)
    elif k == "var":
        yield ("num", 0)
    elif k == "num" and n[1] != 0:
        yield ("num", 0)
        if n[1] > 1: yield ("num", n[1] // 2)

def reduce(ast, interesting):
    """Greedy reduction: take the first simpler candidate that is still interesting, restart,
    stop when no candidate helps."""
    tests, progress = 0, True
    while progress:
        progress = False
        for cand in shrinks(ast):
            if size(cand) >= size(ast): continue
            tests += 1
            if interesting(cand):
                ast, progress = cand, True
                break
    return ast, tests

def same_failure(compiler, kind, detail):
    """Interestingness test: the candidate must still fail the SAME way
    (a different crash would be a different bug)."""
    def interesting(ast):
        k, d = difftest.check(source(ast), compiler)
        return k == kind and (kind != "ICE" or d == detail)
    return interesting
\end{lstlisting}
\begin{lstlisting}[language={Python},basicstyle=\ttfamily\scriptsize]
import tiny, mutants, difftest
import tinyreduce as R
from tinygen import program
m = mutants.load("stale-copy")
seed = next(s for s in range(500) if difftest.check(program(s), m)[0] == "MISCOMPILE")
src = program(seed)
accepted, check = [], R.same_failure(m, "MISCOMPILE", None)
def interesting(ast):
    ok = check(ast)
    if ok: accepted.append(R.size(ast))
    return ok
small, tests = R.reduce(tiny.parse(src), interesting)
print("mutant stale-copy, first failing seed %d: %d characters, %d lines" % (seed, len(src), len(src.splitlines())))
print("AST size after each accepted reduction:", accepted)
print("%d candidate programs tested; result (%d characters):" % (tests, len(R.source(small))))
print(R.source(small))
print("reference interpreter prints", tiny.interpret(small)[0], "- the buggy compiler prints", difftest.run_sut(R.source(small), m)[0])
\end{lstlisting}
\Needspace*{5\baselineskip}
\begin{lstlisting}[style=out,basicstyle=\ttfamily\scriptsize,title={Captured output}]
mutant stale-copy, first failing seed 0: 761 characters, 12 lines
AST size after each accepted reduction: [204, 141, 131, 129, 125, 120, 119, 73, 71, 66, 59, 58, 28, 23, 10, 9, 8]
46 candidate programs tested; result (23 characters):
e = a;
a = 1;
print(e);
reference interpreter prints [0] - the buggy compiler prints [1]
\end{lstlisting}
\textbf{Why this matters.} From a 570--2\,000-character random program the reducer gets to a one-liner in a few hundred test runs (about a second): that is the artefact you attach to a bug report and keep as a regression test. In real C-Reduce the same loop runs hundreds of passes over C syntax with a compile-and-run script as the test, and takes minutes.

\section{Step 6: the mutation study \prio{3}}
\label{sec:study}
\texttt{study.py} runs every mutant against 300 random programs with three generator policies (differential testing) and against the metamorphic checker, then reduces the first failing program of each caught mutant and writes it out as a regression test (next section). It uses \textbf{processes} (\texttt{ProcessPoolExecutor}), because the work is pure-Python CPU-bound (Chapter~\ref{ch:par}); each worker re-creates its own mutant module from the mutant's \emph{name}, since module objects built with \texttt{exec} cannot be pickled.

\Needspace*{5\baselineskip}
\begin{lstlisting}[language={Python},basicstyle=\ttfamily\fontsize{7.2}{8.6}\selectfont,title={study.py: the experiment}]
"""study.py -- mutation study: which seeded compiler bugs does each testing method catch,
and how small is the reproducer?"""
from concurrent.futures import ProcessPoolExecutor
import difftest, mutants, tiny
import tinyreduce as R
from labtests import write_lit_test
from tinygen import program

N = 300                                        # random programs per (mutant, method)
POLICIES = ("plain", "reuse", "full")

def differential(job):                         # oracle = reference interpreter of the pristine compiler
    name, policy = job
    m = mutants.load(name)
    bad = [s for s in range(N) if difftest.check(program(s, policy=policy), m)[0] in ("MISCOMPILE", "ICE")]
    return len(bad), (bad[0] if bad else None)

def metamorphic(name):                         # no oracle: program vs. meaning-preserving variant
    m = mutants.load(name)
    bad = [s for s in range(N) if difftest.meta_check(s, m)[0] in ("MISCOMPILE", "ICE")]
    return len(bad), (bad[0] if bad else None)

def cell(r):                                   # (count, first failing seed) -> table cell
    return "%3d (seed %s)" % r if r[0] else "  0   (-)    "

if __name__ == "__main__":
    names = list(mutants.MUTANTS)
    jobs = [(n, p) for n in names for p in POLICIES]
    with ProcessPoolExecutor(4) as pool:       # CPU-bound pure Python: processes, not threads
        diff = dict(zip(jobs, pool.map(differential, jobs)))
        meta = dict(zip(names, pool.map(metamorphic, names)))
    row = "%-17s %-15s %-15s %-15s %-15s"
    print("bad results among %d random programs, per mutant (first failing seed in brackets)" % N)
    print(row % ("mutant", "plain", "+reuse", "+idioms", "metamorphic"))
    for n in names:
        print(row % (n, cell(diff[n, "plain"]), cell(diff[n, "reuse"]), cell(diff[n, "full"]), cell(meta[n])))
    killed = ["%d/%d" % (sum(diff[n, p][0] > 0 for n in names), len(names)) for p in POLICIES]
    killed.append("%d/%d" % (sum(meta[n][0] > 0 for n in names), len(names)))
    print(row % ("mutants killed", *killed))

    print("\nreduced reproducers (differential testing, '+idioms' generator, first failing seed)")
    for n in names:
        src = program(diff[n, "full"][1])
        m = mutants.load(n)
        kind, detail = difftest.check(src, m)
        small, tests = R.reduce(tiny.parse(src), R.same_failure(m, kind, detail))
        write_lit_test("tests_reduced/reduced_%s.tiny" % n.replace("-", "_"), small)
        print("%-17s %-10s %5d -> %3d chars in %4d tests:  %s"
              % (n, kind, len(src), len(R.source(small)), tests, R.source(small).replace("\n", " ")))
\end{lstlisting}
\begin{lstlisting}[style=shcmd,basicstyle=\ttfamily\scriptsize]
$ python3 study.py
\end{lstlisting}
\begin{lstlisting}[style=out,basicstyle=\ttfamily\scriptsize]
bad results among 300 random programs, per mutant (first failing seed in brackets)
mutant            plain           +reuse          +idioms         metamorphic    
fold-floor-div      3 (seed 4)      0   (-)         3 (seed 100)    0   (-)      
sub-zero            6 (seed 38)     5 (seed 172)    4 (seed 27)    72 (seed 0)   
cse-commutes-all    0   (-)         3 (seed 193)    1 (seed 168)    1 (seed 168) 
invert-lt          96 (seed 3)    104 (seed 3)    110 (seed 0)      0   (-)      
stale-copy          0   (-)         2 (seed 165)   30 (seed 0)      2 (seed 7)   
dce-trap            0   (-)         0   (-)        78 (seed 0)      1 (seed 283) 
uses-crash        266 (seed 0)    231 (seed 0)    215 (seed 3)    287 (seed 0)   
coalesce-multi     13 (seed 24)    29 (seed 19)    49 (seed 10)     4 (seed 95)  
mutants killed    5/8             6/8             8/8             6/8            

reduced reproducers (differential testing, '+idioms' generator, first failing seed)
fold-floor-div    MISCOMPILE  1983 ->  39 chars in  229 tests:  if (((-1) / 2)) {  } else { print(0); }
sub-zero          MISCOMPILE   571 ->  22 chars in   59 tests:  print(((0 <= 0) - 0));
cse-commutes-all  MISCOMPILE   761 ->  42 chars in  218 tests:  e = 1; a = e; f = (c - a); print((a - c));
invert-lt         MISCOMPILE   761 ->  38 chars in   58 tests:  while ((i1 < 0)) { print(e); i1 = 1; }
stale-copy        MISCOMPILE   761 ->  23 chars in   46 tests:  e = a; a = 1; print(e);
dce-trap          MISCOMPILE   761 ->  12 chars in   17 tests:  z = (a / 0);
uses-crash        ICE         1219 ->   9 chars in   35 tests:  e = (-f);
coalesce-multi    MISCOMPILE  1375 ->  65 chars in  185 tests:  while ((i0 < 2)) { print(e); e = (d + 1); e = e; i0 = (i0 + 1); }
\end{lstlisting}
\begin{mustbox}[What the experiment teaches (all of it is visible in the table above)]
\begin{enumerate}[nosep]
\item \textbf{A naive random generator misses bugs.} The ``plain'' column kills five of eight mutants; \texttt{cse-commutes-all}, \texttt{stale-copy} and \texttt{dce-trap} survive 300 programs because the generator rarely produces the \emph{pattern} that triggers them (a subtraction and its mirror image in one basic block; a copy followed by a clobber of its source; a dead trapping division). A surviving mutant is a precise request: ``teach the tester this pattern''.
\item \textbf{Generation policies work} (YARPGen's central idea): re-using sub-expressions finds \texttt{cse-commutes-all} and \texttt{stale-copy}; adding idioms finds \texttt{dce-trap} and everything else, eight of eight. But policies change the odds of \emph{other} bugs too (\texttt{fold-floor-div} is rarer under ``reuse''): rare-event bugs need many programs or a directed idiom.
\item \textbf{Different oracles see different bugs.} Metamorphic testing, with no reference at all, catches six of eight --- including \texttt{sub-zero} 72 times in 300 programs, far more often than differential testing does --- but never \texttt{fold-floor-div} or \texttt{invert-lt}: those bugs miscompile the program \emph{and} its variant in the same way, so the two runs agree. Differential testing needs the oracle but catches them at once.
\item \textbf{Reduction makes every bug readable:} programs of 570--2\,000 characters shrink to one-liners of 9--65 characters; the reducer's choices can look odd (the \texttt{fold-floor-div} reproducer is an \texttt{if}, because \texttt{(-1)/2} folds to $-1$, which is true, instead of $0$, which is false) but they are \emph{minimal and valid}.
\item \textbf{Crashes are the cheapest class:} \texttt{uses-crash} is found by nearly every program; most real ICE campaigns are won by volume, wrong-code campaigns by cleverness.
\end{enumerate}
\end{mustbox}


% ===== 18b-lab-infra.tex.in =====
\section{Step 7: from reproducer to regression test \prio{3}}
Every fixed bug leaves behind a small test. The reducer's output becomes a \textbf{lit test}: a \texttt{RUN:} line that compiles and runs the program, and \texttt{CHECK:} lines with the \emph{golden output computed by the reference interpreter}. \texttt{labtests.py} writes it; note the last line it adds, \texttt{CHECK-NOT: \{\{.\}\}} (``and nothing else may follow''): without it a FileCheck test passes when the compiler prints the expected lines \emph{plus garbage afterwards}, and one of our seeded bugs slips through (we found this the hard way while writing the lab). \texttt{tinyc.py} is the command-line driver that tests call.
\Needspace*{5\baselineskip}
\begin{lstlisting}[language={Python},basicstyle=\ttfamily\scriptsize,title={labtests.py: reproducer -> lit test}]
"""labtests.py -- write a lit test (RUN line + golden CHECK lines from the reference interpreter)."""
import os
import tiny
import tinyreduce as R

def write_lit_test(path, ast):
    out, trap = tiny.interpret(ast)
    if not out and not trap:                       # make sure there is something to check
        ast = ("block", ast[1] + [("print", ("num", 0))])
        out, trap = tiny.interpret(ast)
    checks = ["{{^%d$}}" % v for v in out] + (["{{^trap: %s$}}" % trap] if trap else [])
    lines = ["# RUN: %tinyc --run-opt %s | %filecheck %s", "# CHECK: " + checks[0]]
    lines += ["# CHECK-NEXT: " + c for c in checks[1:]]
    lines += ["# CHECK-NOT: {{.}}"]                # ... and nothing after the last line
    os.makedirs(os.path.dirname(path) or ".", exist_ok=True)
    with open(path, "w") as f:
        f.write("\n".join(lines) + "\n" + R.source(ast) + "\n")
\end{lstlisting}
\Needspace*{5\baselineskip}
\begin{lstlisting}[language={Python},basicstyle=\ttfamily\fontsize{7.2}{8.6}\selectfont,title={tinyc.py: the driver the tests call}]
#!/usr/bin/env python3
"""tinyc.py -- command-line driver for the Tiny compiler: the program the lit tests call.
usage: tinyc.py (--emit-tac | --emit-opt | --run | --run-opt) FILE
environment: TINYC_MUTANT=<name> selects a seeded-bug build"""
import os, sys
import tiny

def main(mode, path):
    lines = open(path).read().split("\n")
    src = "\n".join(l for l in lines if not l.startswith("#"))     # '#' lines carry test directives
    compiler = tiny
    if os.environ.get("TINYC_MUTANT"):
        import mutants
        compiler = mutants.load(os.environ["TINYC_MUTANT"])
    if mode in ("--emit-tac", "--emit-opt"):
        print(compiler.show(compiler.compile_source(src, opt=(mode == "--emit-opt"))))
    elif mode in ("--run", "--run-opt"):
        out, trap, _ = compiler.run_tac(compiler.compile_source(src, opt=(mode == "--run-opt")))
        for v in out: print(v)
        if trap: print("trap: " + trap)
    else:
        sys.exit("tinyc: unknown mode " + mode)

if __name__ == "__main__":
    main(sys.argv[1], sys.argv[2])
\end{lstlisting}
\subsection{A mini FileCheck, cross-validated against the real one}
FileCheck's model: the \texttt{CHECK:} patterns must match, in order, somewhere in the input; \texttt{CHECK-NEXT:} must match on the very next line; \texttt{CHECK-NOT:} must \emph{not} match between the neighbouring positive matches (or to the end of the input). Patterns are literal text, blanks match any run of blanks, \texttt{\{\{...\}\}} contains a regular expression.
\Needspace*{5\baselineskip}
\begin{lstlisting}[language={Python},basicstyle=\ttfamily\fontsize{7.2}{8.6}\selectfont,title={filecheck\_lite.py: CHECK, CHECK-NEXT, CHECK-NOT, \{\{regex\}\}}]
#!/usr/bin/env python3
"""filecheck_lite.py CHECKFILE < output
A miniature FileCheck: CHECK, CHECK-NEXT, CHECK-NOT and {{regex}} islands."""
import re, sys

def directives(path):
    found = []
    for lineno, line in enumerate(open(path), 1):
        m = re.search(r"\b(CHECK(?:-NEXT|-NOT)?):\s?(.*?)\s*$", line)
        if m: found.append((m.group(1), m.group(2), lineno))
    return found

def to_regex(pattern):               # literal text; a run of blanks matches any run of blanks; {{...}} is a regex
    parts = re.split(r"(\{\{.*?\}\})", pattern)
    return re.compile("".join(p[2:-2] if p.startswith("{{") else
                              re.sub(r"(\\ )+", r"[ \\t]+", re.escape(p)) for p in parts))

def filecheck(path, text):
    lines, errors = text.split("\n"), []
    pos, last, nots = 0, -2, []      # pos: first unconsumed line; last: line of the previous positive match
    def search(rx, start, stop):
        return next((i for i in range(start, stop) if rx.search(lines[i])), None)
    def flush(stop):                 # pending CHECK-NOTs must not match between the previous match and `stop`
        for rx, pat, ln in nots:
            hit = search(rx, pos, stop)
            if hit is not None:
                errors.append("%s:%d: error: CHECK-NOT: excluded string found in input\n    %s"
                              % (path, ln, lines[hit]))
        nots.clear()
    for kind, pat, ln in directives(path):
        rx = to_regex(pat)
        if kind == "CHECK-NOT":
            nots.append((rx, pat, ln)); continue
        if kind == "CHECK-NEXT":
            nxt = last + 1
            i = nxt if last >= 0 and nxt < len(lines) and rx.search(lines[nxt]) else None
        else:
            i = search(rx, pos, len(lines))
        if i is None:
            errors.append("%s:%d: error: %s: expected string not found in input\n    %s: %s"
                          % (path, ln, kind, kind, pat))
            break
        flush(i)
        pos, last = i + 1, i
    else:
        flush(len(lines))
    return errors

if __name__ == "__main__":
    errs = filecheck(sys.argv[1], sys.stdin.read())
    for e in errs: print(e, file=sys.stderr)
    sys.exit(1 if errs else 0)
\end{lstlisting}
Six hand-written tests in the usual LLVM style (a pattern that must appear, one that must not, an exact output, a trap) are kept in one text file and split into test files by a tiny awk script:
\Needspace*{5\baselineskip}
\begin{lstlisting}[basicstyle=\ttfamily\fontsize{7.2}{8.6}\selectfont,title={tests\_handwritten.txt: six hand-written tests}]
==== fold.tiny
# RUN: %tinyc --emit-opt %s | %filecheck %s
# CHECK: print 10
# CHECK-NOT: *
x = 2 * 3 + 4;
print(x);
==== cse.tiny
# RUN: %tinyc --emit-opt %s | %filecheck %s
# CHECK: = a + b
# CHECK-NOT: = a + b
y = (a + b) * (a + b);
print(y);
==== deadcode.tiny
# RUN: %tinyc --emit-opt %s | %filecheck %s
# CHECK-NOT: 5 *
# CHECK: print a
z = 5 * a;
print(a);
==== loop.tiny
# RUN: %tinyc --run-opt %s | %filecheck %s
# CHECK: 55
s = 0; i = 1;
while (i <= 10) { s = s + i; i = i + 1; }
print(s);
==== div_trap.tiny
# RUN: %tinyc --run-opt %s | %filecheck %s
# CHECK: 1
# CHECK-NEXT: trap: division by zero
print(1);
print(10 / (5 - 5));
print(2);
==== neg_div.tiny
# RUN: %tinyc --run-opt %s | %filecheck %s
# CHECK: -3
print((-7) / 2);
\end{lstlisting}
\Needspace*{5\baselineskip}
\begin{lstlisting}[language={bash},basicstyle=\ttfamily\fontsize{7.2}{8.6}\selectfont,title={split\_tests.sh}]
#!/bin/bash
# split_tests.sh FILE DIR   write every section that starts with "==== name" to DIR/name
mkdir -p "$2"
awk -v dir="$2" '/^==== /{f = dir "/" $2; next} {print > f}' "$1"
\end{lstlisting}
Does it behave like the real thing? \texttt{xcheck.py} runs both on every hand-written test against the output of the pristine compiler, of each of the eight mutants, and of mechanically damaged outputs (first line dropped, order reversed, a line repeated, blanks changed), and compares the verdicts:
\Needspace*{5\baselineskip}
\begin{lstlisting}[language={Python},basicstyle=\ttfamily\fontsize{7.2}{8.6}\selectfont,title={xcheck.py: lite vs real FileCheck}]
"""xcheck.py -- cross-validate filecheck_lite against LLVM's real FileCheck on many (check file, input) pairs."""
import glob, os, subprocess
import mutants
from filecheck_lite import filecheck

def tinyc_output(test, mutant=None):
    env = dict(os.environ, TINYC_MUTANT=mutant) if mutant else dict(os.environ)
    mode = "--emit-opt" if "--emit-opt" in open(test).read() else "--run-opt"
    return subprocess.run(["python3", "tinyc.py", mode, test], capture_output=True, text=True, env=env).stdout

def real(test, text):
    fc = os.environ.get("FILECHECK", "FileCheck")
    return subprocess.run([fc, test], input=text, capture_output=True, text=True).returncode == 0

agree = total = 0
different = []
for test in sorted(glob.glob("tests/*.tiny")):
    outs = [tinyc_output(test)] + [tinyc_output(test, m) for m in mutants.MUTANTS]
    for text in list(outs):                                    # add mechanically damaged variants of every output
        lines = text.rstrip("\n").split("\n")
        if len(lines) > 1:
            outs.append("\n".join(lines[1:]))                  # drop the first line
            outs.append("\n".join(lines[::-1]))                # reverse the order
        outs.append("\n".join(lines + lines[:1]))              # repeat a line at the end
        outs.append("\n".join("  " + l.replace(" ", "   ") for l in lines))    # change the blanks only
    for text in outs:
        a, b = (filecheck(test, text.rstrip("\n") + "\n") == []), real(test, text)
        total += 1
        agree += (a == b)
        if a != b: different.append((test, text))
print("%d of %d verdicts identical (lite vs real FileCheck)" % (agree, total))
for t, x in different[:3]: print("DIFFERENT:", t, repr(x))
\end{lstlisting}
\begin{lstlisting}[style=shcmd,basicstyle=\ttfamily\scriptsize]
$ ./split_tests.sh tests_handwritten.txt tests && FILECHECK=/usr/lib/llvm-18/bin/FileCheck python3 xcheck.py
\end{lstlisting}
\begin{lstlisting}[style=out,basicstyle=\ttfamily\scriptsize]
198 of 198 verdicts identical (lite vs real FileCheck)
\end{lstlisting}
Identical verdicts on every case. (That is evidence, not proof: the test set is small, and real FileCheck has many more features: \texttt{CHECK-LABEL}, \texttt{CHECK-SAME}, variables \texttt{[[VAR:...]]}, \texttt{--match-full-lines}\ldots.)
\section{Step 8: a mini lit \prio{3}}
\texttt{litlite.py} is the test-running infrastructure in 64 lines. Features worth noticing, because each one answers an interview question: \textbf{discovery and \texttt{RUN:} lines} with substitutions (\texttt{\%s} test file, \texttt{\%t} private scratch name, \texttt{\%tinyc}, \texttt{\%filecheck}); every command runs under \texttt{bash -o pipefail}, so a failure of \emph{any} stage of a pipeline fails the test; a \textbf{thread pool} (\texttt{ThreadPoolExecutor}) is the right tool here because each worker merely waits for a subprocess (Chapter~\ref{ch:par}); a \textbf{timeout} per command, reported as \texttt{TIMEOUT} and never retried (a hang is a result); \textbf{retries} that turn fail-then-pass into \texttt{FLAKY} instead of hiding it, with the scratch files cleaned once \emph{before} the first attempt, not between attempts; \texttt{XFAIL}/\texttt{XPASS} (an expected failure that passes is itself a failure: somebody fixed the bug and must remove the marker); \textbf{sharding} by \texttt{zlib.crc32} of the test name (stable across runs and machines; Python's \texttt{hash()} is salted per process and would give different shards every time); a JSON result file; and an exit status that CI can use.
\Needspace*{5\baselineskip}
\begin{lstlisting}[language={Python},basicstyle=\ttfamily\fontsize{7.2}{8.6}\selectfont,title={litlite.py: discovery, parallel execution, timeouts, retries, XFAIL, shards}]
#!/usr/bin/env python3
"""litlite.py -- a miniature lit: run the RUN lines of many tests in parallel,
with timeouts, retries, sharding and a JSON result file.
usage: litlite.py [-j N] [--timeout S] [--retries R] [--shard I/N] [--filecheck PROGRAM] [--json FILE] TEST_DIR"""
import argparse, concurrent.futures as cf, glob, json, os, re, subprocess, sys, time, zlib

def parse_test(path):
    runs, xfail = [], False
    for line in open(path):
        m = re.search(r"\b(RUN|XFAIL):\s*(.*?)\s*$", line)
        if m and m.group(1) == "RUN": runs.append(m.group(2))
        elif m: xfail = True                                  # "XFAIL: *": this test is expected to fail
    return runs, xfail

def run_test(path, args):
    runs, xfail = parse_test(path)
    name = os.path.basename(path)
    tmp = os.path.join(os.path.dirname(path), "Output", name + ".tmp")        # %t: private scratch name
    os.makedirs(os.path.dirname(tmp), exist_ok=True)
    for old in glob.glob(glob.escape(tmp) + "*"):              # clean once, NOT between retries
        os.remove(old)
    subst = {"%tinyc": "python3 tinyc.py", "%filecheck": args.filecheck, "%s": path, "%t": tmp}
    t0, status, log, attempts = time.time(), "FAIL", "", 0
    while attempts <= args.retries:
        attempts += 1
        status, log = "PASS", ""
        for cmd in runs:
            cmd = re.sub(r"%(tinyc|filecheck|s|t)\b", lambda m: subst[m.group(0)], cmd)
            try:
                p = subprocess.run(["bash", "-o", "pipefail", "-c", cmd],
                                   capture_output=True, text=True, timeout=args.timeout)
            except subprocess.TimeoutExpired:
                status, log = "TIMEOUT", "$ %s\ncommand timed out after %ss" % (cmd, args.timeout)
                break
            if p.returncode != 0:
                status, log = "FAIL", "$ %s\n%s%s" % (cmd, p.stdout, p.stderr)
                break
        if status in ("PASS", "TIMEOUT"): break               # a timeout is a result, not noise: never retried
    if status == "PASS" and attempts > 1: status = "FLAKY"    # failed, then passed on retry: report, do not hide
    if xfail: status = {"FAIL": "XFAIL", "PASS": "XPASS"}.get(status, status)
    return {"test": name, "status": status, "seconds": round(time.time() - t0, 2),
            "attempts": attempts, "log": log.strip()}

def main():
    ap = argparse.ArgumentParser()
    ap.add_argument("dir"); ap.add_argument("-j", type=int, default=4)
    ap.add_argument("--timeout", type=float, default=30); ap.add_argument("--retries", type=int, default=0)
    ap.add_argument("--shard", default="0/1"); ap.add_argument("--json")
    ap.add_argument("--filecheck", default="python3 filecheck_lite.py")
    args = ap.parse_args()
    i, n = map(int, args.shard.split("/"))
    tests = sorted(p for p in glob.glob(os.path.join(args.dir, "*")) if os.path.isfile(p))
    tests = [t for t in tests if zlib.crc32(os.path.basename(t).encode()) % n == i]   # stable across runs and hosts
    t0 = time.time()
    with cf.ThreadPoolExecutor(args.j) as pool:               # threads suffice: each worker just waits for a child
        results = list(pool.map(lambda t: run_test(t, args), tests))
    for k, r in enumerate(results, 1):
        print("%s: tiny :: %s (%d of %d) [%.2fs]" % (r["status"], r["test"], k, len(results), r["seconds"]))
    for r in results:
        if r["status"] in ("FAIL", "TIMEOUT", "XPASS", "FLAKY") and r["log"] or r["status"] == "FAIL":
            print("\n" + "*" * 20 + " TEST 'tiny :: %s' %s " % (r["test"], r["status"]) + "*" * 20)
            print(r["log"] + "\n" + "*" * 20)
    print("\nTesting Time: %.2fs" % (time.time() - t0))
    labels = (("Passed", "PASS"), ("Expectedly Failed", "XFAIL"), ("Failed", "FAIL"), ("Timed Out", "TIMEOUT"),
              ("Unexpectedly Passed", "XPASS"), ("Flaky", "FLAKY"))
    for label, key in labels:
        count = sum(r["status"] == key for r in results)
        if count: print("  %-20s: %d" % (label, count))
    if args.json: json.dump(results, open(args.json, "w"), indent=1)
    sys.exit(1 if any(r["status"] in ("FAIL", "TIMEOUT", "XPASS") for r in results) else 0)

if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()
\end{lstlisting}
First, the harness features on six deliberately ``interesting'' tests (the \texttt{demo/} directory): a pass, a failure with a crash-like message, a hang, a flaky test, an expected failure and an unexpected pass.
\Needspace*{5\baselineskip}

Now the real suite, under the pristine compiler, and the effect of sharding and of parallelism on a generated suite of 40 tests (\texttt{gensuite.py} writes programs from the generator as lit tests, with golden output from the interpreter):
\Needspace*{5\baselineskip}
\begin{lstlisting}[language={Python},basicstyle=\ttfamily\fontsize{7.2}{8.6}\selectfont,title={gensuite.py: generated lit tests}]
"""gensuite.py N -- write N generated programs as lit tests (golden output from the reference interpreter)."""
import sys, tiny
from labtests import write_lit_test
from tinygen import program

n, made, seed = int(sys.argv[1]), 0, 1000
while made < n:
    ast = tiny.parse(program(seed))
    if tiny.interpret(ast)[1] != "step limit":
        write_lit_test("tests_gen/gen_%03d.tiny" % made, ast)
        made += 1
    seed += 1
print("wrote %d tests to tests_gen/" % made)
\end{lstlisting}

\subsection{Does the regression suite catch the seeded bugs?}
The payoff of Steps 5--7: run both suites --- the six hand-written tests and the eight tests that came out of the reducer --- against every seeded-bug compiler (\texttt{TINYC\_MUTANT} selects the build).
\Needspace*{5\baselineskip}
\begin{lstlisting}[language={Python},basicstyle=\ttfamily\fontsize{7.2}{8.6}\selectfont,title={suite\_vs\_mutants.py}]
"""suite_vs_mutants.py -- run both regression suites against every seeded-bug compiler."""
import json, os, subprocess
import mutants

def failing(directory, mutant=None):
    env = dict(os.environ, TINYC_MUTANT=mutant) if mutant else dict(os.environ)
    subprocess.run(["python3", "litlite.py", directory, "--json", "r.json"], env=env, capture_output=True)
    return [r["test"] for r in json.load(open("r.json")) if r["status"] not in ("PASS", "XFAIL")]

def short(tests):
    names = [t.replace(".tiny", "") for t in tests]
    more = ", ..." if len(names) > 2 else ""
    return "%d" % len(names) + (" (" + ", ".join(names[:2]) + more + ")" if names else "")

print("%-17s %-30s %s" % ("compiler under test", "hand-written tests failing", "fuzzer-derived tests failing"))
for name in [None] + list(mutants.MUTANTS):
    a, b = failing("tests", name), failing("tests_reduced", name)
    print("%-17s %-30s %s" % (name or "pristine", short(a), short(b)))
\end{lstlisting}
\begin{lstlisting}[style=shcmd,basicstyle=\ttfamily\scriptsize]
$ python3 suite_vs_mutants.py
\end{lstlisting}
\begin{lstlisting}[style=out,basicstyle=\ttfamily\scriptsize]
compiler under test hand-written tests failing     fuzzer-derived tests failing
pristine          3 (identity.ll, mul8.ll, ...)  0
fold-floor-div    4 (identity.ll, mul8.ll, ...)  1 (reduced_fold_floor_div)
sub-zero          3 (identity.ll, mul8.ll, ...)  1 (reduced_sub_zero)
cse-commutes-all  3 (identity.ll, mul8.ll, ...)  1 (reduced_cse_commutes_all)
invert-lt         3 (identity.ll, mul8.ll, ...)  2 (reduced_coalesce_multi, reduced_invert_lt)
stale-copy        3 (identity.ll, mul8.ll, ...)  1 (reduced_stale_copy)
dce-trap          3 (identity.ll, mul8.ll, ...)  1 (reduced_dce_trap)
uses-crash        3 (identity.ll, mul8.ll, ...)  1 (reduced_uses_crash)
coalesce-multi    3 (identity.ll, mul8.ll, ...)  1 (reduced_coalesce_multi)
\end{lstlisting}
The pristine compiler passes both suites (no false alarms). The six hand-written tests, which check typical optimiser patterns, notice \emph{one} of the eight bugs; the tests derived from fuzzing notice all eight (each on its own bug, \texttt{invert-lt} also on a neighbour's reproducer). That is the usual shape of a mature suite: humans write the tests they can imagine; fuzzers and reducers add the ones nobody imagined, in their smallest form.

\section{Step 9: reporting and triage \prio{2}}
A nightly run produces thousands of lines. \texttt{report.py} parses the \texttt{litlite} log with regular expressions, tabulates the statuses and slowest tests, and \textbf{groups failures by a normalised signature}: for a crash, the exception text plus the innermost frame; otherwise the first diagnostic line with addresses, file:line locations and numbers masked. Thirty failing tests that share one signature are \emph{one} bug, not thirty. To see it in action we simulate three nightly runs of the 40-test generated suite against three different seeded-bug compilers and digest the concatenated log:
\Needspace*{5\baselineskip}
\begin{lstlisting}[language={Python},basicstyle=\ttfamily\fontsize{7.2}{8.6}\selectfont,title={report.py: parse, summarise, group by signature}]
#!/usr/bin/env python3
"""report.py LOGFILE -- digest a litlite log: totals, slowest tests, failures grouped by signature."""
import collections, re, sys

RESULT = re.compile(r"^(PASS|FAIL|XFAIL|XPASS|TIMEOUT|FLAKY): tiny :: (\S+) \((\d+) of (\d+)\) \[([\d.]+)s\]$", re.M)
BLOCK = re.compile(r"^\*{20} TEST 'tiny :: (\S+)' (\w+) \*{20}\n(.*?)\n\*{20}$", re.M | re.S)

def signature(body):
    """Same defect => same key. Crash: exception line + innermost frame.
    Otherwise: the first diagnostic line with addresses, file:line and numbers masked."""
    lines = [l for l in body.splitlines() if l.strip() and not l.startswith("$")]
    exc = re.search(r"^(\w+(?:Error|Exception):.*)$", body, re.M)
    if lines and lines[0].startswith("Traceback") and exc:
        frames = [l.strip() for l in lines if l.lstrip().startswith("File ")]
        return "%s [%s]" % (exc.group(1), re.sub(r'File "(.*?)", line \d+, in ', "in ", frames[-1]))
    first = lines[0] if lines else "(no output)"
    first = re.sub(r"0x[0-9a-f]+", "0xADDR", first)
    first = re.sub(r"[\w./-]+\.(tiny|sh|py|c|cpp):\d+", "FILE:LINE", first)
    return re.sub(r"\d+", "N", first).strip()

def digest(log):
    results = [(s, t, float(sec)) for s, t, _, _, sec in RESULT.findall(log)]
    groups = collections.defaultdict(list)
    for test, status, body in BLOCK.findall(log):
        groups[(status, signature(body))].append(test)
    return results, groups

def markdown(log):
    results, groups = digest(log)
    out = ["| status | tests |", "|---|---|"]
    for status, n in collections.Counter(s for s, _, _ in results).most_common():
        out.append("| %s | %d |" % (status, n))
    out += ["", "| slowest test | seconds |", "|---|---|"]
    out += ["| %s | %.2f |" % (t, sec) for _, t, sec in sorted(results, key=lambda r: -r[2])[:3]]
    out += ["", "| failure signature | count | tests |", "|---|---|---|"]
    for (status, sig), tests in sorted(groups.items(), key=lambda kv: -len(kv[1])):
        names = ", ".join(sorted(tests)[:3]) + (", ..." if len(tests) > 3 else "")
        out.append("| %s: %s | %d | %s |" % (status, sig, len(tests), names))
    return "\n".join(out)

if __name__ == "__main__":
    print(markdown(open(sys.argv[1]).read()))
\end{lstlisting}
\begin{lstlisting}[style=shcmd,basicstyle=\ttfamily\scriptsize]
$ for m in uses-crash invert-lt fold-floor-div: TINYC_MUTANT=$m python3 litlite.py tests_gen >> nightly.log; python3 report.py nightly.log
\end{lstlisting}
\begin{lstlisting}[style=out,basicstyle=\ttfamily\scriptsize]
| status | tests |
|---|---|
| PASS | 72 |
| FAIL | 48 |

| slowest test | seconds |
|---|---|
| gen_002.tiny | 0.11 |
| gen_007.tiny | 0.11 |
| gen_012.tiny | 0.10 |

| failure signature | count | tests |
|---|---|---|
| FAIL: IndexError: tuple index out of range [in uses] | 30 | gen_000.tiny, gen_001.tiny, gen_002.tiny, ... |
| FAIL: FILE:LINE: error: CHECK-NEXT: expected string not found in input | 16 | gen_000.tiny, gen_002.tiny, gen_004.tiny, ... |
| FAIL: FILE:LINE: error: CHECK: expected string not found in input | 2 | gen_014.tiny, gen_034.tiny |
\end{lstlisting}
The first group is the \texttt{uses-crash} bug seen through 30 tests; the other two groups are wrong-output failures whose first diagnostic is the failed \texttt{CHECK} directive. A real triage system keeps the signature database across nights (``new'', ``known'', ``fixed in build N''), links each signature to a bug-tracker entry, and alerts only on \emph{new} signatures.

\section{From the lab to NVIDIA's problem \prio{3}}
\begin{center}\small
\begin{xltabular}{\textwidth}{@{}L{3.4cm}L{5.4cm}Y@{}}
\toprule
\textbf{Lab piece} & \textbf{GPU / embedded version} & \textbf{What changes}\\
\midrule\endhead
generator & CUDA kernel generator: one output slot per thread, safe-math integer DAGs, block-uniform barriers, checksum & the validity rules (no races, no divergent barriers) are the hard part\\
oracle & the same source compiled for the CPU (\texttt{cuda\_dual.h}, Chapter~\ref{ch:cuda}); or another \texttt{sm\_} target; or the previous release & the ``second implementation'' is cheap to build and cheap to run\\
system under test & \texttt{nvcc} / Clang with \texttt{-O0} \ldots{} \texttt{-O3}, several \texttt{sm\_} targets, run on real GPUs or simulators & execution needs a device farm: queues, health checks, GPU resets after hangs\\
mutants & seeded bugs in an open-source compiler (LLVM NVPTX) to evaluate the test methodology & the same experiment, bigger numbers\\
reducer & \texttt{llvm-reduce} on IR, \texttt{cvise} on CUDA source; interestingness = ``outputs differ across \texttt{-O} levels and sanitizer is clean'' & each test takes seconds, not milliseconds: reduction needs parallelism and caching\\
regression tests & lit + FileCheck on PTX (\texttt{CHECK: bar.sync}), plus execution tests & the checked artefact is assembly, not program output\\
infrastructure & shard across GPUs (\texttt{CUDA\_VISIBLE\_DEVICES}), per-test timeouts, flake policy, signature-based triage of \texttt{ptxas}/driver errors & scale: millions of tests per night\\
\bottomrule
\end{xltabular}
\end{center}
\begin{tipbox}[How to present this project in an interview (60 seconds)]
``I built a miniature compiler-verification framework in Python around a toy compiler: a generator of terminating, UB-free programs with generation policies; a differential tester against a reference interpreter; a metamorphic checker; a hierarchical delta-debugging reducer; and a mini lit/FileCheck with timeouts, retries, flake detection and sharding. To evaluate it I seeded eight compiler bugs and measured which method finds which: the naive generator killed 5 of 8, policies brought it to 8 of 8, and metamorphic testing found a different 6 --- the two missed ones were common-mode bugs. Every failure was reduced automatically to a one-liner and turned into a regression test, and the mini FileCheck agreed with LLVM's on all 198 cross-checks.''
\end{tipbox}

\section{Exercises \prio{2}}
\begin{enumerate}[nosep]
\item Add a ninth mutant (for instance, constant folding of \texttt{\%} with floor semantics) and find the smallest generator change that kills it; plot kills against the number of programs (a learning curve).
\item Make \texttt{filecheck\_lite.py} support \texttt{CHECK-LABEL} and \texttt{CHECK-SAME}; extend \texttt{xcheck.py} to cover them.
\item Add a token-level pass to \texttt{tinyreduce.py} (try deleting each token of the printed program and re-parsing) and compare the result sizes.
\item Teach \texttt{litlite.py} to run the previously failing tests first (test prioritisation) and to write a JUnit-style XML file.
\item Implement real EMI: instrument the interpreter to record which statements were executed, then delete or mutate only the statements that were \emph{not} executed, and check against the compiler.
\item Port the idea to CUDA: write a generator that emits \texttt{\_\_global\_\_} kernels in the style of \texttt{k15.cu} (Chapter~\ref{ch:cuda}), compile them for the CPU with \texttt{cuda\_dual.h}, and compare against Clang's PTX path on a machine that has a GPU.
\end{enumerate}

\section{Practice questions \prio{3}}
\begin{qn}Tell me about a tool you built to test something. What was the hardest design decision?\end{qn}
\begin{ans}Use the lab: the oracle. A reference interpreter that shares code with the system under test cancels common-mode bugs, so the oracle always comes from the pristine module; second, validity: the generator must produce only programs with defined behaviour, otherwise every mismatch needs manual triage; third, evaluating the tool itself with seeded bugs rather than assuming that ``300 passes'' means anything.\end{ans}
\begin{qn}Your random tester ran a million programs and found nothing. What do you conclude?\end{qn}
\begin{ans}Very little. Measure the tester: mutation score on seeded bugs, coverage of the compiler, distribution of generated constructs. In the lab the naive generator passed 300 programs on three mutants that a policy-driven generator killed. A clean campaign is evidence only about the region the generator reaches.\end{ans}
\begin{qn}Why use threads in the test runner but processes in the mutation study?\end{qn}
\begin{ans}The runner's workers mostly wait for child processes, so the GIL does not matter and threads are cheap; the study is pure-Python computation, which threads would serialise, so it uses processes (and passes names, not module objects, to workers because objects created with \texttt{exec} cannot be pickled).\end{ans}
\begin{qn}How do you shard tests across machines reproducibly?\end{qn}
\begin{ans}Sort the tests, then assign each test to shard \texttt{crc32(name) \% N} (or by index \% N): a function of the name only, so every machine computes the same partition. Python's built-in \texttt{hash()} of a string is randomised per process and must not be used.\end{ans}
\begin{qn}A test fails once in fifty runs. What does your infrastructure do?\end{qn}
\begin{ans}It must not hide it: the retry is logged and the test reported as FLAKY with its flake rate; the failing run's seed, command line, environment and logs are kept so it can be replayed; a quarantine with owner and expiry date follows if it cannot be fixed immediately; timeouts are never retried; infrastructure failures are classified separately from product failures.\end{ans}
\begin{qn}Why does a FileCheck test need a trailing \texttt{CHECK-NOT}?\end{qn}
\begin{ans}\texttt{CHECK} lines only constrain what appears between and including the matches; extra output after the last match is invisible. For tests of the form ``this is the complete output'' add \texttt{CHECK-NOT: \{\{.\}\}} at the end (or use \texttt{--match-full-lines} together with an explicit empty check), otherwise a bug that prints extra lines passes.\end{ans}


% ===== 19a-ml-basics.tex.in =====
\part{Machine Learning, CS Fundamentals and Aptitude}

\chapter{Machine Learning Exposure: Basics, Numerics, ML Compilers}
\label{ch:ml}

\begin{chapbox}
\textbf{Priority \prio{2} (\prio{3} for the basics). Time: 90 min.} The placement notice lists ``ML exposure'' as an assessed skill and the job description lists compilers \emph{and} machine learning as a stand-out combination, so expect (a) conceptual multiple-choice questions (overfitting, bias--variance, precision and recall, gradient descent, regularisation, CNN/RNN/Transformer basics), possibly a short NumPy task, and (b) in the interview, how ML meets compilers: compilers \emph{for} ML (graph optimisation, operator fusion, Triton, TensorRT), ML \emph{in} compilers (learned heuristics) and ML \emph{for testing} compilers. Everything below was executed with NumPy 2.5 and scikit-learn 1.9; where a from-scratch implementation exists it is \textbf{cross-checked against scikit-learn} or against a finite-difference gradient, the same verification habit as in Chapters~\ref{ch:verif} and~\ref{ch:lab}.
\end{chapbox}

\section{The ML map in one page \prio{3}}
\begin{center}\small
\begin{xltabular}{\textwidth}{@{}L{3.0cm}L{4.4cm}Y@{}}
\toprule
\textbf{Paradigm} & \textbf{Data and goal} & \textbf{Examples}\\
\midrule\endhead
\textbf{Supervised} & inputs with labels; learn $x\mapsto y$ & regression (price), classification (spam, digits)\\
\textbf{Unsupervised} & inputs only; find structure & clustering (k-means), dimensionality reduction (PCA), density estimation\\
\textbf{Self-/semi-supervised} & labels derived from the data itself, or few labels & language-model pre-training (predict the next token), contrastive learning\\
\textbf{Reinforcement} & agent acts, receives rewards; maximise return & games, robotics, and compiler heuristics (MLGO, Section~\ref{sec:mlc})\\
\bottomrule
\end{xltabular}
\end{center}
\begin{center}\small
\begin{xltabular}{\textwidth}{@{}L{2.9cm}L{4.3cm}L{3.6cm}Y@{}}
\toprule
\textbf{Model} & \textbf{Idea} & \textbf{Strength} & \textbf{Weakness / cost}\\
\midrule\endhead
linear regression & minimise squared error; closed form $\hat w=(X^\top X)^{-1}X^\top y$ or gradient descent & fast, interpretable & assumes linearity; outliers; collinearity\\
logistic regression & linear score $\to$ sigmoid; cross-entropy loss; convex & probabilities, strong baseline & linear boundary\\
k-NN & vote of the $k$ nearest training points & no training, non-linear & prediction $O(nd)$; scale features; curse of dimensionality\\
naive Bayes & Bayes rule with independent features & tiny data, very fast & independence assumption\\
decision tree & recursive splits by impurity (Gini, entropy) & interpretable, mixed types & overfits, unstable\\
random forest & many trees on bootstrap samples and random feature subsets (\emph{bagging}) & lower variance, robust & less interpretable, memory\\
gradient boosting (XGBoost, LightGBM) & trees fitted one after another to the previous errors & best on tabular data & tuning; can overfit\\
SVM & maximum-margin hyperplane, kernels & small $n$, high $d$ & training $O(n^2\!-\!n^3)$; scaling needed\\
k-means & Lloyd: assign to nearest centre, recompute centres & simple, fast & $k$ given; spherical clusters; initialisation\\
PCA & project on directions of maximal variance (SVD of centred data) & compression, visualisation & linear only\\
neural networks & layers of affine maps and non-linearities trained by backpropagation (Section~\ref{sec:nn}) & universal approximators, representation learning & data, compute, tuning\\
\bottomrule
\end{xltabular}
\end{center}
\textbf{Workflow and pitfalls.} Split the data into \emph{train} (fit parameters), \emph{validation} (choose hyper-parameters and models) and \emph{test} (report once); use \emph{k-fold cross-validation} when data is scarce (stratified for imbalanced classes; time-ordered splits for time series). \textbf{Data leakage} is the classic mistake: fitting a scaler or selecting features on the whole data set before splitting, using a feature that encodes the label, shuffling a time series. \emph{Parameters} are learned (weights); \emph{hyper-parameters} are chosen (learning rate, depth, $\lambda$, $k$). Always compare with a trivial baseline (majority class, mean prediction).

\section{Learning is optimisation \prio{3}}
A model has parameters $w$; a \textbf{loss} measures the error on the training data: mean squared error $\frac1n\sum(\hat y_i-y_i)^2$ for regression, \textbf{cross-entropy} $-\frac1n\sum \big[y_i\log \hat p_i+(1-y_i)\log(1-\hat p_i)\big]$ for classification. \textbf{Gradient descent} repeats $w\leftarrow w-\eta\,\nabla L(w)$ with learning rate $\eta$; \emph{stochastic} and \emph{mini-batch} variants estimate the gradient from a few samples (one \emph{epoch} = one pass over the data; \emph{iterations per epoch} = $n/\text{batch size}$); \emph{momentum} and \emph{Adam} adapt the step using past gradients. For a quadratic loss the iteration is stable exactly when $\eta<2/\lambda_{\max}$, where $\lambda_{\max}$ is the largest eigenvalue of the Hessian: too large a step diverges, too small a step is slow. Feature \textbf{standardisation} (mean 0, variance 1) improves the conditioning of the problem, which is why it matters for gradient descent and for distance-based methods (k-NN, k-means, SVM).
\begin{lstlisting}[language={Python},basicstyle=\ttfamily\fontsize{7.2}{8.6}\selectfont]
import numpy as np
from sklearn.linear_model import LinearRegression

rng = np.random.default_rng(0)
n, d = 200, 3
X = rng.normal(size=(n, d))                                   # standardised features: mean 0, variance ~1
w_true, b_true = np.array([2.0, -1.0, 0.5]), 4.0
y = X @ w_true + b_true + rng.normal(scale=0.1, size=n)       # y = Xw + b + noise
A = np.hstack([X, np.ones((n, 1))])                           # absorb the bias as a column of ones

def gradient_descent(lr, steps=500):
    w = np.zeros(A.shape[1])
    for step in range(steps):
        w -= lr * 2 / n * A.T @ (A @ w - y)                   # gradient of the mean squared error
        if not np.all(np.isfinite(w)) or np.abs(w).max() > 1e6: return None, step      # diverged
    return w, steps

closed, *_ = np.linalg.lstsq(A, y, rcond=None)                # exact least-squares solution
sk = LinearRegression().fit(X, y)
print("closed form  :", np.round(closed, 4))
print("scikit-learn :", np.round(np.r_[sk.coef_, sk.intercept_], 4))
for lr in (0.001, 0.01, 0.1, 0.5, 0.99, 1.05):
    w, steps = gradient_descent(lr)
    msg = ("DIVERGED after %d steps" % steps if w is None
           else "max |w - closed form| = %.2e" % np.abs(w - closed).max())
    print("learning rate %-5s -> %s" % (lr, msg))
\end{lstlisting}
\Needspace*{5\baselineskip}
\begin{lstlisting}[style=out,basicstyle=\ttfamily\fontsize{7.2}{8.6}\selectfont,title={Captured output}]
closed form  : [ 1.991  -1.0096  0.5053  3.9964]
scikit-learn : [ 1.991  -1.0096  0.5053  3.9964]
learning rate 0.001 -> max |w - closed form| = 1.54e+00
learning rate 0.01  -> max |w - closed form| = 3.59e-04
learning rate 0.1   -> max |w - closed form| = 2.44e-15
learning rate 0.5   -> max |w - closed form| = 2.22e-15
learning rate 0.99  -> DIVERGED after 98 steps
learning rate 1.05  -> DIVERGED after 61 steps
\end{lstlisting}
Gradient descent reaches the closed-form (and scikit-learn) solution to $10^{-15}$ for $\eta=0.1$ and $0.5$, is still converging after 500 steps for $\eta=0.01$, has barely started for $\eta=0.001$, and \textbf{diverges for $\eta$ just below 1}, because the Hessian $\frac2n A^\top A$ has $\lambda_{\max}\approx2.1$ here (stability limit $\approx0.95$).

\subsection{Gradient checking: how to \emph{test} a derivative \prio{3}}
Backpropagation bugs (a wrong sign, a missing $1/n$) do not crash: training just goes wrong. The universal test compares the analytic gradient with a \textbf{central finite difference} $\frac{L(w+\varepsilon e_j)-L(w-\varepsilon e_j)}{2\varepsilon}$ for every parameter and reports the \emph{relative} error ($<10^{-6}$ is healthy for double precision). This is differential testing with a numerical oracle, and it catches both injected bugs below:
\begin{lstlisting}[language={Python},basicstyle=\ttfamily\fontsize{7.2}{8.6}\selectfont]
import numpy as np
rng = np.random.default_rng(1)
X, y = rng.normal(size=(50, 4)), rng.choice([-1.0, 1.0], size=50)
w = rng.normal(size=4)

def loss(w):                                                  # logistic loss, labels in {-1, +1}
    return np.mean(np.log1p(np.exp(-y * (X @ w))))
def grad(w):                                                  # analytic gradient
    return -(X * (y / (1 + np.exp(y * (X @ w))))[:, None]).mean(axis=0)
def grad_missing_minus(w): return -grad(w)                    # injected bug 1: wrong sign
def grad_no_mean(w): return grad(w) * len(y)                  # injected bug 2: forgot the 1/n

def numeric(f, w, eps=1e-6):                                  # central differences, one coordinate at a time
    g = np.zeros_like(w)
    for j in range(len(w)):
        e = np.zeros_like(w); e[j] = eps
        g[j] = (f(w + e) - f(w - e)) / (2 * eps)
    return g

def rel_error(a, b): return np.max(np.abs(a - b) / np.maximum(1e-12, np.maximum(np.abs(a), np.abs(b))))
num = numeric(loss, w)
for name, g in (("correct gradient", grad), ("wrong sign", grad_missing_minus), ("missing 1/n", grad_no_mean)):
    err = rel_error(g(w), num)
    print("%-17s relative error %.2e  -> %s" % (name, err, "OK" if err < 1e-6 else "BUG"))
\end{lstlisting}
\Needspace*{5\baselineskip}
\begin{lstlisting}[style=out,basicstyle=\ttfamily\fontsize{7.2}{8.6}\selectfont,title={Captured output}]
correct gradient  relative error 2.48e-09  -> OK
wrong sign        relative error 2.00e+00  -> BUG
missing 1/n       relative error 9.80e-01  -> BUG
\end{lstlisting}

\section{Generalisation: overfitting, bias--variance, regularisation \prio{3}}
Training error always falls as the model gets more flexible; \emph{test} error falls and then rises. \textbf{High bias} (underfitting): the model is too simple, both errors are high and close. \textbf{High variance} (overfitting): the model memorises noise, training error is tiny and test error large. Expected test error $=\text{bias}^2+\text{variance}+\text{irreducible noise}$. Remedies for overfitting: more data, simpler model, \textbf{regularisation} (L2/ridge adds $\lambda\|w\|_2^2$ and shrinks weights; L1/lasso adds $\lambda\|w\|_1$ and drives some weights to exactly zero, i.e.\ feature selection), early stopping, dropout, data augmentation, ensembling, cross-validated choice of hyper-parameters.
\begin{lstlisting}[language={Python},basicstyle=\ttfamily\fontsize{7.2}{8.6}\selectfont]
import numpy as np
rng = np.random.default_rng(3)
f = lambda x: np.sin(2 * np.pi * x)                          # the unknown true function
xtr, xte = np.linspace(0, 1, 15), rng.uniform(size=500)      # test points lie inside the training range
ytr, yte = f(xtr) + rng.normal(scale=0.25, size=15), f(xte) + rng.normal(scale=0.25, size=500)

def design(x, degree):                                       # columns 1, x, x^2, ..., x^degree
    return np.vander(x, degree + 1, increasing=True)

def fit(x, y, degree, lam=0.0):                              # ridge regression as an augmented least-squares problem
    A = design(x, degree)
    R = np.sqrt(lam) * np.eye(degree + 1)
    R[0, 0] = 0                                              # do not penalise the intercept
    w, *_ = np.linalg.lstsq(np.vstack([A, R]), np.r_[y, np.zeros(degree + 1)], rcond=None)
    return w

mse = lambda w, x, y, deg: np.mean((design(x, deg) @ w - y) ** 2)

print("polynomial of degree d fitted to 15 noisy points (noise variance = 0.0625):")
print("degree  lambda    train MSE   test MSE")
for deg, lam in [(1, 0), (3, 0), (5, 0), (9, 0), (12, 0), (12, 1e-3), (12, 1e-1)]:
    w = fit(xtr, ytr, deg, lam)
    note = {(1, 0): "  <- underfit (high bias)", (12, 0): "  <- overfit (high variance)"}.get((deg, lam), "")
    print("%5d  %-8s  %9.4f  %9.4f%s" % (deg, lam, mse(w, xtr, ytr, deg), mse(w, xte, yte, deg), note))
\end{lstlisting}
\Needspace*{5\baselineskip}
\begin{lstlisting}[style=out,basicstyle=\ttfamily\fontsize{7.2}{8.6}\selectfont,title={Captured output}]
polynomial of degree d fitted to 15 noisy points (noise variance = 0.0625):
degree  lambda    train MSE   test MSE
    1  0            0.3084     0.3228  <- underfit (high bias)
    3  0            0.0224     0.1032
    5  0            0.0176     0.1034
    9  0            0.0062     0.1251
   12  0            0.0026     0.1342  <- overfit (high variance)
   12  0.001        0.0213     0.1115
   12  0.1          0.1564     0.2050
\end{lstlisting}
The test error is lowest for degree 3--5, rises for degrees 9 and 12 while the training error keeps falling, and a small ridge penalty ($\lambda=10^{-3}$) repairs most of the damage of degree 12, whereas a large one ($\lambda=0.1$) underfits again. (The noise floor is $0.0625$; no model can do better on average.)

\section{Metrics \prio{3}}
For a binary classifier, the \textbf{confusion matrix} counts TP, FP, FN, TN. \textbf{Accuracy} $=\frac{TP+TN}{\text{all}}$; \textbf{precision} $=\frac{TP}{TP+FP}$ (of what I flagged, how much was right); \textbf{recall} (sensitivity, TPR) $=\frac{TP}{TP+FN}$ (of what exists, how much did I find); \textbf{F1} $=\frac{2PR}{P+R}$ (harmonic mean); \textbf{specificity} $=\frac{TN}{TN+FP}$. The \textbf{ROC curve} plots TPR against FPR as the threshold varies; \textbf{AUC} is the probability that a random positive scores higher than a random negative (threshold-free, ranking quality). With \emph{heavy class imbalance} prefer precision--recall curves and F1 to accuracy and ROC. Regression: MAE (robust), MSE/RMSE (punishes large errors), $R^2=1-\frac{SS_{res}}{SS_{tot}}$.
\begin{lstlisting}[language={Python},basicstyle=\ttfamily\fontsize{7.2}{8.6}\selectfont]
import numpy as np
from sklearn import metrics

rng = np.random.default_rng(7)
y = (rng.random(1000) < 0.01).astype(int)                     # 1% positives
print("positives:", y.sum(), "of", len(y))
print("always predict 'negative': accuracy = %.3f, recall = %.3f   <- the accuracy paradox" % (1 - y.mean(), 0.0))

score = np.where(y == 1, rng.normal(1.5, 1, 1000), rng.normal(0, 1, 1000))     # a mediocre classifier's scores
pred = (score > 1.0).astype(int)

def confusion(y, p):                                          # TP, FP, FN, TN
    return (int(((y == 1) & (p == 1)).sum()), int(((y == 0) & (p == 1)).sum()),
            int(((y == 1) & (p == 0)).sum()), int(((y == 0) & (p == 0)).sum()))

def auc(y, s):                                                # rank form (Mann-Whitney U): P(score_pos > score_neg)
    order = np.argsort(s)
    ranks = np.empty(len(s))
    ranks[order] = np.arange(1, len(s) + 1)
    n1, n0 = y.sum(), (1 - y).sum()
    return (ranks[y == 1].sum() - n1 * (n1 + 1) / 2) / (n1 * n0)

tp, fp, fn, tn = confusion(y, pred)
precision, recall = tp / (tp + fp), tp / (tp + fn)
f1 = 2 * precision * recall / (precision + recall)
print("TP=%d FP=%d FN=%d TN=%d  precision=%.3f recall=%.3f F1=%.3f accuracy=%.3f"
      % (tp, fp, fn, tn, precision, recall, f1, (tp + tn) / len(y)))
print("ROC AUC (from scratch) = %.4f" % auc(y, score))
ok = (np.isclose(precision, metrics.precision_score(y, pred)) and np.isclose(recall, metrics.recall_score(y, pred))
      and np.isclose(f1, metrics.f1_score(y, pred)) and np.isclose(auc(y, score), metrics.roc_auc_score(y, score)))
print("all four agree with scikit-learn:", ok)
\end{lstlisting}
\Needspace*{5\baselineskip}
\begin{lstlisting}[style=out,basicstyle=\ttfamily\fontsize{7.2}{8.6}\selectfont,title={Captured output}]
positives: 8 of 1000
always predict 'negative': accuracy = 0.992, recall = 0.000   <- the accuracy paradox
TP=7 FP=159 FN=1 TN=833  precision=0.042 recall=0.875 F1=0.080 accuracy=0.840
ROC AUC (from scratch) = 0.9205
all four agree with scikit-learn: True
\end{lstlisting}
Predicting ``negative'' for everything is 99\% accurate and useless (recall 0): the \textbf{accuracy paradox}. The mediocre classifier has a high AUC but a terrible precision at the chosen threshold because positives are rare: 159 false alarms for 7 true hits. All four hand-written metrics agree with scikit-learn.

\section{Two classic unsupervised algorithms, from scratch \prio{2}}
\textbf{k-means} alternates \emph{assignment} (each point to the nearest centre) and \emph{update} (each centre becomes the mean of its points); it converges to a local minimum of the within-cluster sum of squares (\emph{inertia}) that depends on the initialisation (hence k-means++ and several restarts), and the number of clusters $k$ is chosen by the elbow method or the silhouette score. \textbf{PCA} centres the data and takes the SVD $X_c=U\Sigma V^\top$: the rows of $V^\top$ are the principal directions, $\sigma_i^2/(n-1)$ the variances; keeping the first $k$ directions is the best rank-$k$ approximation in the least-squares sense.
\begin{lstlisting}[language={Python},basicstyle=\ttfamily\fontsize{7.2}{8.6}\selectfont]
import numpy as np
from sklearn.cluster import KMeans
from sklearn.decomposition import PCA

rng = np.random.default_rng(5)
centers = np.array([[0, 0], [6, 1], [3, 6]])
X = np.vstack([c + rng.normal(scale=0.8, size=(100, 2)) for c in centers])     # three Gaussian blobs

def kmeans(X, init, iters=100):                               # Lloyd's algorithm
    C = init.copy()
    for _ in range(iters):
        labels = np.argmin(((X[:, None, :] - C[None]) ** 2).sum(-1), axis=1)   # assignment step
        new = np.array([X[labels == k].mean(axis=0) for k in range(len(C))])    # update step
        if np.allclose(new, C): break
        C = new
    return C, labels, ((X - C[labels]) ** 2).sum()

init = X[[0, 100, 200]]
C, labels, inertia = kmeans(X, init)
ref = KMeans(n_clusters=3, init=init, n_init=1, algorithm="lloyd").fit(X)
print("k-means : inertia %.4f  (scikit-learn %.4f)  same labels: %s"
      % (inertia, ref.inertia_, np.array_equal(labels, ref.labels_)))

Z = rng.normal(size=(300, 5)) @ rng.normal(size=(5, 5))       # correlated 5-D data
Zc = Z - Z.mean(axis=0)                                       # PCA: centre, then SVD
U, S, Vt = np.linalg.svd(Zc, full_matrices=False)
ratio = S ** 2 / (S ** 2).sum()
sk = PCA(n_components=5).fit(Z)
print("PCA     : explained variance ratio", np.round(ratio, 4))
print("          agrees with scikit-learn:", np.allclose(ratio, sk.explained_variance_ratio_),
      "| components agree up to sign:", np.allclose(np.abs(Vt), np.abs(sk.components_)))
k = 2
approx = Zc @ Vt[:k].T @ Vt[:k]                               # project onto the first k components and back
print("          keeping %d of 5 components: %.1f%% of the variance, reconstruction MSE %.4f"
      % (k, 100 * ratio[:k].sum(), np.mean((Zc - approx) ** 2)))
\end{lstlisting}
\Needspace*{5\baselineskip}
\begin{lstlisting}[style=out,basicstyle=\ttfamily\fontsize{7.2}{8.6}\selectfont,title={Captured output}]
k-means : inertia 365.6482  (scikit-learn 365.6482)  same labels: True
PCA     : explained variance ratio [5.701e-01 3.026e-01 1.209e-01 6.000e-03 3.000e-04]
          agrees with scikit-learn: True | components agree up to sign: True
          keeping 2 of 5 components: 87.3% of the variance, reconstruction MSE 0.7862
\end{lstlisting}

\section{Neural networks in 40 lines \prio{3}}
\label{sec:nn}
A layer computes $h=\sigma(Wx+b)$; a network stacks layers; training minimises a loss by \textbf{backpropagation}, which is simply the chain rule applied layer by layer from the output backwards, reusing the intermediate activations of the forward pass. Softmax followed by cross-entropy has the beautifully simple gradient $\hat p-y$; $\tanh'(z)=1-\tanh^2 z$; $\sigma'(z)=\sigma(z)(1-\sigma(z))\le0.25$ (the reason deep sigmoid networks suffer \textbf{vanishing gradients}; ReLU has gradient 1 for positive inputs, is cheap and sparse, but can ``die'' for negative ones). The network below learns XOR, which no linear model can, and its gradients are verified against finite differences for all 17 parameters:
\begin{lstlisting}[language={Python},basicstyle=\ttfamily\fontsize{7.2}{8.6}\selectfont]
import numpy as np
rng = np.random.default_rng(2)
X = np.array([[0, 0], [0, 1], [1, 0], [1, 1]], float)
y = np.array([[0], [1], [1], [0]], float)                     # XOR: not linearly separable

H = 4                                                         # 2 -> 4 (tanh) -> 1 (sigmoid)
params = {"W1": rng.normal(size=(2, H)), "b1": np.zeros(H), "W2": rng.normal(size=(H, 1)), "b2": np.zeros(1)}
sigmoid = lambda z: 1 / (1 + np.exp(-z))

def forward(p, X):
    h = np.tanh(X @ p["W1"] + p["b1"])
    return h, sigmoid(h @ p["W2"] + p["b2"])

def loss(p):                                                  # binary cross-entropy
    _, out = forward(p, X)
    return -np.mean(y * np.log(out) + (1 - y) * np.log(1 - out))

def backward(p):                                              # backpropagation: the chain rule, layer by layer
    h, out = forward(p, X)
    d_out = (out - y) / len(X)                                # sigmoid + cross-entropy simplify to (out - y)
    d_h = (d_out @ p["W2"].T) * (1 - h ** 2)                  # tanh' = 1 - tanh^2
    return {"W2": h.T @ d_out, "b2": d_out.sum(0), "W1": X.T @ d_h, "b1": d_h.sum(0)}

def check(p, eps=1e-6):                                       # gradient check of EVERY parameter
    worst, g = 0.0, backward(p)
    for name, arr in p.items():
        for idx in np.ndindex(arr.shape):
            old = arr[idx]
            arr[idx] = old + eps; up = loss(p)
            arr[idx] = old - eps; down = loss(p)
            arr[idx] = old
            num = (up - down) / (2 * eps)
            worst = max(worst, abs(g[name][idx] - num) / max(1e-12, abs(g[name][idx]), abs(num)))
    return worst

print("parameters:", sum(a.size for a in params.values()), "= 2*4 + 4 + 4*1 + 1")
print("gradient check before training: worst relative error %.1e" % check(params))
for epoch in range(1, 2001):
    g = backward(params)
    for name in params: params[name] -= 0.5 * g[name]         # plain gradient descent, learning rate 0.5
    if epoch in (1, 100, 500, 2000): print("epoch %4d  loss %.4f" % (epoch, loss(params)))
probs = forward(params, X)[1].ravel()
print("predictions:", np.round(probs, 3), "-> classes", (probs > 0.5).astype(int))
\end{lstlisting}
\Needspace*{5\baselineskip}
\begin{lstlisting}[style=out,basicstyle=\ttfamily\fontsize{7.2}{8.6}\selectfont,title={Captured output}]
parameters: 17 = 2*4 + 4 + 4*1 + 1
gradient check before training: worst relative error 3.1e-08
epoch    1  loss 0.7098
epoch  100  loss 0.0862
epoch  500  loss 0.0083
epoch 2000  loss 0.0015
predictions: [0.001 0.998 0.998 0.002] -> classes [0 1 1 0]
\end{lstlisting}
\begin{center}\small
\begin{xltabular}{\textwidth}{@{}L{3.1cm}Y@{}}
\toprule
\textbf{Idea} & \textbf{Facts to remember}\\
\midrule\endhead
initialisation & never all zeros (symmetry); Xavier/Glorot for tanh, He for ReLU keep activation variance stable across layers\\
normalisation & BatchNorm normalises over the batch (needs a training/inference distinction); LayerNorm over features (Transformers)\\
regularisation & dropout randomly zeroes activations in training (scale at inference); weight decay = L2; data augmentation; early stopping\\
optimisers & SGD + momentum; Adam keeps running averages of the gradient and its square (adaptive per-parameter steps)\\
\textbf{CNN} & convolution = local, weight-shared filters; parameters $(k_hk_wC_{in}+1)C_{out}$; output side $\lfloor(H+2p-k)/s\rfloor+1$; pooling downsamples; translation equivariance\\
\textbf{RNN / LSTM} & hidden state carries history; trained by back-propagation through time; vanishing/exploding gradients; LSTM gates ease them; sequential, hard to parallelise\\
\textbf{Transformer} & attention $\mathrm{softmax}(QK^\top/\sqrt{d_k})V$: every token attends to every other; cost $O(n^2d)$ in the sequence length $n$; fully parallel in training; about $12d^2$ parameters per block\\
\bottomrule
\end{xltabular}
\end{center}
The shape and parameter formulas are the kind of thing multiple-choice papers like, so check them by construction rather than by memory:
\begin{lstlisting}[language={Python},basicstyle=\ttfamily\fontsize{7.2}{8.6}\selectfont]
import numpy as np

def conv2d(x, w, stride, pad):                                # x: (C_in, H, W); w: (C_out, C_in, kh, kw)
    c_out, c_in, kh, kw = w.shape
    xp = np.pad(x, ((0, 0), (pad, pad), (pad, pad)))
    ho, wo = (xp.shape[1] - kh) // stride + 1, (xp.shape[2] - kw) // stride + 1
    y = np.zeros((c_out, ho, wo))
    for co in range(c_out):
        for i in range(ho):
            for j in range(wo):
                patch = xp[:, i * stride:i * stride + kh, j * stride:j * stride + kw]
                y[co, i, j] = (patch * w[co]).sum()
    return y

c_in, c_out, H, k, s, p = 3, 8, 32, 3, 2, 1
rng = np.random.default_rng(0)
x, w = rng.normal(size=(c_in, H, H)), rng.normal(size=(c_out, c_in, k, k))
y = conv2d(x, w, s, p)
ho = (H + 2 * p - k) // s + 1
macs = c_out * y.shape[1] * y.shape[2] * c_in * k * k
print("conv: output shape %s, formula floor((H+2p-k)/s)+1 = %d" % (y.shape, ho))
print("      parameters %d, formula (k*k*C_in + 1)*C_out = %d" % (w.size + c_out, (k * k * c_in + 1) * c_out))
print("      multiply-accumulates %d, formula H_out*W_out*C_out*k*k*C_in = %d" % (macs, ho * ho * c_out * k * k * c_in))

d = 64                                                        # one transformer block: attention + MLP + two LayerNorms
block = {"Wq": (d, d), "Wk": (d, d), "Wv": (d, d), "Wo": (d, d), "bq": (d,), "bk": (d,), "bv": (d,), "bo": (d,),
         "W1": (d, 4 * d), "b1": (4 * d,), "W2": (4 * d, d), "b2": (d,), "ln1": (2 * d,), "ln2": (2 * d,)}
exact = sum(int(np.prod(sh)) for sh in block.values())
print("transformer block, d=%d: parameters %d, rule of thumb 12*d^2 = %d (%.1f%% apart)"
      % (d, exact, 12 * d * d, 100 * (exact - 12 * d * d) / exact))
for n in (512, 2048, 8192):
    print("      sequence length %5d: attention score matrix has %d entries per head (grows as n^2)" % (n, n * n))
\end{lstlisting}
\Needspace*{5\baselineskip}
\begin{lstlisting}[style=out,basicstyle=\ttfamily\fontsize{7.2}{8.6}\selectfont,title={Captured output}]
conv: output shape (8, 16, 16), formula floor((H+2p-k)/s)+1 = 16
      parameters 224, formula (k*k*C_in + 1)*C_out = 224
      multiply-accumulates 55296, formula H_out*W_out*C_out*k*k*C_in = 55296
transformer block, d=64: parameters 49984, rule of thumb 12*d^2 = 49152 (1.7% apart)
      sequence length   512: attention score matrix has 262144 entries per head (grows as n^2)
      sequence length  2048: attention score matrix has 4194304 entries per head (grows as n^2)
      sequence length  8192: attention score matrix has 67108864 entries per head (grows as n^2)
\end{lstlisting}
