\section{Practice questions \prio{2}}
\begin{qn}What is the range of an 8-bit two's-complement integer, and what is $0\mathrm{x}80$ sign-extended to 32 bits?\end{qn}
\begin{ans}$-128\ldots127$; $0\mathrm{xFFFFFF80}$ (see the number-system output above).\end{ans}
\begin{qn}Why is \texttt{0.1 + 0.2 == 0.3} false in double precision but true in single precision?\end{qn}
\begin{ans}None of the three has an exact binary representation; in binary64 the rounding errors of $0.1$ and $0.2$ add up to a value one ulp away from the double nearest to $0.3$; in binary32 the errors happen to cancel after rounding (the script prints \texttt{False} and \texttt{True}). The lesson: never compare floating-point results with \texttt{==}; use a tolerance.\end{ans}
\begin{qn}A cache is 32~KB, 8-way set associative with 64-byte blocks and 32-bit addresses. How many tag, index and offset bits?\end{qn}
\begin{ans}Offset 6 bits (64 B), sets $=32768/(64\cdot8)=64\Rightarrow$ index 6 bits, tag $32-6-6=20$ bits.\end{ans}
\begin{qn}Why is column-major traversal of a row-major matrix slow, and what are two fixes?\end{qn}
\begin{ans}Each access touches a different cache line, so spatial locality is lost and the miss rate approaches 100\% (6.25\% for the row-major walk, simulated above). Fixes: loop interchange (walk rows), blocking/tiling, padding rows when the stride maps many rows to the same sets, or a transposed copy.\end{ans}
\begin{qn}Explain forwarding and the load-use hazard.\end{qn}
\begin{ans}Forwarding (bypassing) feeds an ALU result straight from the EX/MEM latch to the next instruction's ALU input, avoiding the wait for write-back. A load produces its value only at the end of MEM, so an instruction that uses it immediately must stall one cycle even with forwarding.\end{ans}
\begin{qn}Compute the average waiting time for FCFS and SJF for processes (arrival, burst) $(0,8),(1,4),(2,9),(3,5)$.\end{qn}
\begin{ans}FCFS 8.75, non-preemptive SJF 7.75 (preemptive SRTF 6.5): see the simulation above.\end{ans}
\begin{qn}What is Belady's anomaly? Give the reference string.\end{qn}
\begin{ans}Giving FIFO more frames can increase the number of page faults: for 1 2 3 4 1 2 5 1 2 3 4 5, FIFO has 9 faults with 3 frames and 10 with 4. LRU and OPT are stack algorithms and never show it.\end{ans}
\begin{qn}State the four conditions for deadlock and how the Banker's algorithm avoids it.\end{qn}
\begin{ans}Mutual exclusion, hold and wait, no preemption, circular wait. The Banker's algorithm grants a request only if, after granting, a safe sequence still exists (some order in which all processes can obtain their maximum claims and finish); otherwise the requester waits.\end{ans}
\begin{qn}How many processes does \texttt{fork(); fork(); fork();} create? What does \texttt{if (fork() \&\& fork())} do?\end{qn}
\begin{ans}$2^3=8$ processes in total (7 new). In \texttt{fork() \&\& fork()}, only the original parent executes the second \texttt{fork} (the first child sees 0 and short-circuits), so there are 3 processes, and only the parent takes the true branch.\end{ans}
\begin{qn}Why can a \texttt{printf} before \texttt{fork} print twice?\end{qn}
\begin{ans}Output to a pipe or file is fully buffered; the unflushed buffer is part of the process image that \texttt{fork} copies, so both processes flush it at exit. Use \texttt{fflush(stdout)} before \texttt{fork} or end the line when writing to a terminal.\end{ans}
\begin{qn}A 32-bit virtual address space with 4~KB pages and 4-byte page-table entries: size of a single-level page table? Why use two levels?\end{qn}
\begin{ans}$2^{20}$ entries $\times$ 4 B $=4$ MB per process. Two levels (10 + 10 bits) need only the 4 KB top table plus second-level tables for the regions actually used.\end{ans}
\begin{qn}Difference between a mutex and a semaphore; between a process and a thread; between TCP and UDP?\end{qn}
\begin{ans}A mutex has an owner and provides mutual exclusion; a semaphore is a counter of permits that any thread may signal. A process has its own address space; threads of one process share it. TCP provides a reliable ordered byte stream with connection setup and congestion control; UDP sends independent datagrams without guarantees.\end{ans}
\begin{qn}How many usable hosts does 192.168.10.77/26 have, and what is its broadcast address?\end{qn}
\begin{ans}62 usable hosts (network 192.168.10.64, broadcast 192.168.10.127).\end{ans}
\begin{qn}Which normal form does a table violate if a non-key attribute determines another non-key attribute?\end{qn}
\begin{ans}Third normal form (a transitive dependency); decompose into two tables.\end{ans}
\begin{qn}\texttt{SELECT COUNT(*), COUNT(col)} on a column with NULLs: do they differ? Does \texttt{WHERE col = NULL} select the NULL rows?\end{qn}
\begin{ans}They differ: \texttt{COUNT(*)} counts all rows, \texttt{COUNT(col)} only non-NULL values (the SQL output above shows 6 vs.\ 5). \texttt{col = NULL} is never true (it is UNKNOWN); use \texttt{col IS NULL}.\end{ans}


% ===== 21a-aptitude-quant.tex.in =====
\chapter{Aptitude and Puzzles: The Non-Technical Half of the Online Assessment}
\label{ch:apt}

\begin{chapbox}
\textbf{Priority \prio{2}; the formula sheet and the mock test are \prio{3}. Time: 90 minutes (30 for the formula sheet and shortcuts, 20 for reasoning and puzzles, 40 for the timed mock test).} The assessment sheet says ``Mix of Aptitude and Technical (Online) on HackerRank''; the number of aptitude questions, the time limit and the negative-marking rule are \textbf{not published}. Chapter~\ref{ch:intel} reconstructs the probable shape from candidate reports: 10--15 aptitude MCQs in roughly 12 minutes (percentages, ratios, time--work, probability, permutations, series, logical reasoning, a puzzle or two). This chapter prepares that genre and a little beyond it, so that the real section feels easy. The practice items are original (no leaked questions are claimed); every formula was checked against brute force or exact arithmetic, every puzzle answer was found by a search program, and every answer of the 30-question mock test is recomputed by code (Section~\ref{sec:mock}).
\end{chapbox}

\section{How to run the aptitude round \prio{3}}
\begin{mustbox}[Ten rules for timed multiple-choice aptitude]
\begin{enumerate}[nosep,leftmargin=*]
\item \textbf{Budget:} 60--90 seconds per question on average. Mark and skip anything that gives no plan within 30 seconds; the second pass is cheaper because the easy marks are already banked.
\item \textbf{Order:} formula-plug questions first (percentages, averages, series, dice and coins), then multi-step word problems, then puzzles.
\item \textbf{Use the options.} Four options are a free hint about the size and form of the answer; for ratio, age and mixture problems \emph{substitute the options back} instead of solving.
\item \textbf{Estimate before computing:} successive rises of 20\% and 10\% give a little over 30\%; any option that violates the estimate is out.
\item \textbf{Units once, at the start:} km/h $\leftrightarrow$ m/s is $\times\frac5{18}$ or $\times\frac{18}5$; minutes $\leftrightarrow$ hours.
\item \textbf{Read the last line first:} what is asked (difference? percentage? \emph{remaining} work? the smaller angle?).
\item \textbf{Complement:} ``at least one'' $=1-P(\text{none})$; ``not divisible by'' $=$ total $-$ divisible.
\item \textbf{Digits and parity eliminate:} check the last digit, the digit sum (divisibility by 3 and 9) and the parity of every option before computing anything.
\item \textbf{Draw:} a five-second sketch for direction, seating, blood-relation and Venn questions beats mental tracking.
\item \textbf{Negative marking unknown?} Assume it exists: guess only after eliminating two options.
\end{enumerate}
\end{mustbox}

\begin{trapbox}[Eight classic traps (each one is a wrong option in some test)]
\begin{itemize}[nosep]
\item \textbf{Average speed} over equal distances is the \emph{harmonic} mean ($48$, not $50$, for 60 and 40 km/h).
\item \textbf{Percentage up and down are not symmetric:} $+25\%$ needs $-20\%$ to return; $+20\%$ followed by $-20\%$ is $-4\%$.
\item \textbf{Successive discounts do not add:} 20\% and 10\% make 28\%, not 30\%.
\item \textbf{Profit is on the cost; discount is on the marked price.}
\item \textbf{Compound versus simple interest:} the second year earns interest on the first year's interest; CI $-$ SI for two years is $P(r/100)^2$.
\item \textbf{Working together} adds \emph{rates}, not times (12 and 18 days give 7.2 days, not 15).
\item \textbf{Repeated letters:} divide by the factorials of the repeat counts (LEVEL has 30 arrangements, not 120).
\item \textbf{Clock angles} ask for the smaller angle; direction questions ask for distance \emph{and} direction.
\end{itemize}
\end{trapbox}

\begin{tipbox}[On the HackerRank page]
Read the instruction page before the clock starts: whether the sections are separately timed (a finished section may be locked), whether there is negative marking, and which proctoring options are on (webcam, tab-switch and copy-paste monitoring are configurable by the company, and campus drives are often supervised in a lab). Keep pen and paper ready and do not assume that an on-screen calculator exists.
\end{tipbox}

\section{Quantitative formula sheet, every row verified \prio{3}}
Each formula below is followed by a number that appears in the checks of Section~\ref{sec:formcheck}: the formula and a brute-force computation agree.
\begin{center}\small
\begin{xltabular}{\textwidth}{@{}L{2.9cm}Y@{}}
\toprule
\textbf{Topic} & \textbf{Formulas and rules}\\
\midrule\endhead
percentages & $x\%$ of $y$ equals $y\%$ of $x$; successive changes $a\%$ and $b\%$ give a net $a+b+\frac{ab}{100}\%$ (+20\%, +10\% $\to$ +32\%); after a rise of $x\%$ a fall of $\frac{100x}{100+x}\%$ restores the original (+25\% needs $-20\%$); percentage \emph{points} are not percent\\
profit and loss & profit\% $=\frac{SP-CP}{CP}\times100$, always on the \emph{cost} unless stated; $SP=CP(1\pm\frac p{100})$; a discount is on the \emph{marked price}: $SP=MP(1-\frac d{100})$; markup 25\% and discount 10\% give profit 12.5\%; successive discounts $a,b$ give $a+b-\frac{ab}{100}$ (20\% and 10\% $=28\%$); selling 900~g as 1~kg gains $\frac{100}{900}=11.11\%$\\
ratio, mixture, ages & $a:b=c:d\iff ad=bc$; partnership shares $\propto$ capital $\times$ time; ages: put the ratio parts as $3x,5x$ and \emph{substitute the options}; \textbf{alligation}: cheaper price $c_1$, dearer $c_2$, mean $m$ gives quantity ratio $(c_2-m):(m-c_1)$ (Rs~40 and Rs~55 rice mixed to Rs~46: $9:6=3:2$); replacing $x$ litres $n$ times in a vessel of $V$ litres leaves $V(1-\frac xV)^n$ of the original\\
interest & $SI=\frac{PRT}{100}$; $A=P(1+\frac r{100})^n$; \textbf{CI $-$ SI for 2 years} $=P(\frac r{100})^2$ (Rs~5000 at 10\%: Rs~50); SI doubles money in $\frac{100}r$ years; \textbf{rule of 72}: compound doubling in about $72/r$ years; half-yearly compounding uses $\frac r2$ and $2n$; CAGR $=(\text{end}/\text{start})^{1/n}-1$\\
averages & $\text{average}=\frac{\text{sum}}{\text{count}}$; replacing one member changes the sum by the difference; weighted mean; average speed for equal distances $\frac{2xy}{x+y}$ (60 and 40 km/h: 48), for equal times $\frac{x+y}2$\\
speed, distance, time & km/h $\to$ m/s: $\times\frac5{18}$; relative speed \emph{adds} for opposite directions, \emph{subtracts} for the same direction; a train passes a pole in $\frac{L}{v}$ and a platform in $\frac{L+L_p}{v}$ (150~m train, 250~m platform, 20~s: $\frac{400}{20}\times3.6=72$ km/h); two trains: $\frac{L_1+L_2}{v_1\pm v_2}$; boat: downstream $u+v$, upstream $u-v$ (12 km/h boat, 3 km/h stream, 45 km each way: 8 h)\\
work and pipes & rate $=\frac1{\text{days}}$ and rates \emph{add}; two workers: $\frac{ab}{a+b}$ days (12 and 18: 7.2); \textbf{LCM method}: let the job be the LCM of the days (36 units; 3 and 2 units a day) and count units; leaks have negative rate (fill in 6 h, leak empties in 12 h: 12 h together); wages $\propto$ work done; $\frac{M_1D_1H_1}{W_1}=\frac{M_2D_2H_2}{W_2}$\\
divisibility & 2 (last digit even), 3 and 9 (digit sum), 4 (last two digits), 5, 6 (2 and 3), 8 (last three digits), 11 (alternating digit sum), 7 (strip the last digit and subtract twice it from the rest); $abcabc$ is a multiple of $1001=7\cdot11\cdot13$\\
unit digits, remainders & last digits cycle: 2: 2-4-8-6; 3: 3-9-7-1; 7: 7-9-3-1; 8: 8-4-2-6; 4 and 9 alternate; 0, 1, 5, 6 never change; reduce the exponent modulo the cycle length ($7^{123}$ ends in 3 because $123\bmod4=3$); $2^{100}\bmod7=2$ because $2^3\equiv1$\\
factors and sums & $n=p^aq^b\cdots$ has $(a+1)(b+1)\cdots$ divisors (360 $=2^33^25$: 24); $\text{HCF}\times\text{LCM}=$ product of the two numbers; trailing zeros of $n!$ $=\lfloor\frac n5\rfloor+\lfloor\frac n{25}\rfloor+\cdots$ (100!: 24); $\sum k=\frac{n(n+1)}2$, $\sum k^2=\frac{n(n+1)(2n+1)}6$, $\sum k^3=\big(\frac{n(n+1)}2\big)^2$; AP: $S_n=\frac n2\big(2a+(n-1)d\big)$; GP: $S_n=a\frac{r^n-1}{r-1}$; the first $n$ odd numbers sum to $n^2$\\
counting & $n!$ arrangements; with repeats $\frac{n!}{p!\,q!\cdots}$ (LEVEL: $\frac{5!}{2!2!}=30$); $^nP_r=\frac{n!}{(n-r)!}$, $^nC_r=\frac{n!}{r!(n-r)!}$; circular arrangements $(n-1)!$; derangements $D_n\approx\frac{n!}e$ ($D_4=9$); non-negative solutions of $x_1+\cdots+x_k=n$: $\binom{n+k-1}{k-1}$ (stars and bars)\\
probability & $P=\frac{\text{favourable}}{\text{total}}$; $P(\text{at least one})=1-P(\text{none})$; independent events multiply; $P(A\mid B)=\frac{P(A\cap B)}{P(B)}$; Bayes $P(A\mid B)=\frac{P(B\mid A)P(A)}{P(B)}$ (1\% prevalence, 99\% sensitivity, 5\% false positives: a positive test means only $\frac{99}{594}=16.7\%$); two dice: the sum 7 is the most likely ($\frac16$), $P(8)=\frac5{36}$; birthday paradox: 23 people give 50.7\%; expectation is linear\\
clocks, calendars & hour hand $0.5^\circ$/min, minute hand $6^\circ$/min; angle $=|30h-5.5m|$ (use $360-d$ if above 180; 3:40 gives $130^\circ$); the hands overlap 11 times in 12 hours; leap year: divisible by 4, but centuries only if divisible by 400; 100 years contain 5 odd days, 400 years 0\\
mensuration & circle $\pi r^2$, $2\pi r$; sphere $\frac43\pi r^3$, $4\pi r^2$; cone $\frac13\pi r^2h$ with slant height $\sqrt{r^2+h^2}$; cylinder $\pi r^2h$; Heron $\sqrt{s(s-a)(s-b)(s-c)}$ (13-14-15: 84); primitive Pythagorean triples below hypotenuse 30: 3-4-5, 5-12-13, 7-24-25, 8-15-17, 20-21-29; an $n$-gon has $\frac{n(n-3)}2$ diagonals and interior angles summing to $(n-2)180^\circ$\\
\bottomrule
\end{xltabular}
\end{center}

\begin{tipbox}[Data interpretation (tables, bar and pie charts) in five rules]
\begin{enumerate}[nosep,leftmargin=*]
\item Read the title, the \emph{unit} (thousands? per cent?) and the axis labels before the question.
\item \textbf{Percentage change} $=\frac{\text{new}-\text{old}}{\text{old}}$, against the \emph{earlier} value; the largest \emph{absolute} change is often not the largest \emph{percentage} change (question M15 of the mock test is built on this).
\item Compare shares as fractions by cross-multiplying; round to two significant digits unless the options are close.
\item A pie-chart sector has angle $360^\circ\times$ share (25\% is $90^\circ$); a difference of ``percentage points'' is not a percentage difference.
\item Combine group averages with weights (counts), never by averaging the averages.
\end{enumerate}
\end{tipbox}

\subsection{The formula checks \prio{1}}\label{sec:formcheck}
The script behind the sheet pairs each closed-form formula with a brute-force or exact-fraction computation of the same quantity (divisibility rules on 1--5000, unit-digit cycles on 200 exponents, derangements by enumerating permutations, the birthday paradox with exact fractions, a clock-angle formula against geometry at all 720 minutes, the sphere and cone volumes against numerical integration, and so on) and prints one line per check. The list is also a compact index of the facts above.

\section{Calculation shortcuts, and the proof that they work \prio{3}}
\begin{itemize}[nosep]
\item \textbf{Squares ending in 5:} $(10n+5)^2=n(n+1)\,|\,25$, for example $35^2=3\cdot4\,|\,25=1225$.
\item \textbf{Multiply by 11:} write the digits with the sum of each neighbouring pair between them and carry leftwards: $57\times11=5\,|\,12\,|\,7=627$.
\item \textbf{Near a round number:} $97\times103=(100-3)(100+3)=9991$; $n\times99=100n-n$; $n\times101=100n+n$.
\item \textbf{Casting out nines:} the digit root of a product equals the digit root of the product of the digit roots. It can \emph{reject} a wrong option, never confirm a right one.
\item \textbf{Divisibility by 7:} strip the last digit and subtract twice it from the rest, repeat; numbers of the form $abcabc$ are multiples of $7\cdot11\cdot13=1001$.
\item \textbf{Percentages from fractions:} memorise $\frac1n$ for $n=2,\dots,12,16$ (below) and combine: $\frac38=3\times12.5\%=37.5\%$, $\frac57=71.43\%$.
\item \textbf{Rule of 72:} doubling time $\approx72/r$ years; accurate for $r$ between about 6 and 12 per cent, a little off at the ends of the table.
\end{itemize}
\Needspace*{5\baselineskip}

   1/10 = 10.00%     1/11 =  9.09%     1/12 =  8.33%     1/16 =  6.25%

squares 11..25: 121 144 169 196 225 256 289 324 361 400 441 484 529 576 625

powers of 2:    2 4 8 16 32 64 128 256 512 1024 2048 4096

speed tricks, each verified on every case in the stated range:
  (10n+5)^2 = n(n+1) followed by 25, n < 100                   ok
  x 11: digit sums between the digits, carry left, n < 10000   ok
  (m-d)(m+d) = m^2 - d^2:  97 x 103 = 100^2 - 3^2 = 9991       ok

  72/r       36.00   18.00   12.00    9.00    6.00    4.00    3.00
  exact      35.00   17.67   11.90    9.01    6.12    4.19    3.22

Reading the output: the sevenths \emph{rotate} the same six digits ($\frac17=0.\overline{142857}$, $\frac27=0.\overline{285714}$, \ldots), so one memorised cycle gives all six fractions; the 7-test and the casting-out-nines test were run on every number in the stated ranges, not on examples.

\section{Logical reasoning: seven question types \prio{2}}
\begin{center}\small
\begin{xltabular}{\textwidth}{@{}L{3.0cm}Y@{}}
\toprule
\textbf{Type} & \textbf{Method that works under time pressure}\\
\midrule\endhead
number series & write the \textbf{difference table}; if the first differences are not constant look at the second; then try ratios (geometric), $n^2\pm k$ or $n^3\pm k$, Fibonacci type (each term the sum of the two before), differences that are themselves a pattern (powers of 2, primes), and \emph{two interleaved series} (extend the one that the next position belongs to)\\
coding--decoding & positions $A=1,\dots,Z=26$; constant shift; reversal then shift; opposite letter ($A\leftrightarrow Z$: the positions add to 27); find the rule on the \emph{given} word and apply it unchanged to the question word\\
direction sense & draw axes and track $(x,y)$ with north $=+y$, east $=+x$; a right turn rotates the heading clockwise; the answer is a distance (Pythagoras) \emph{and} a direction\\
blood relations & draw a small tree from the speaker; maternal/paternal uncle and aunt are the siblings of the parents; ``the only daughter of my mother'' is the speaker if the speaker is a woman; if the sex is not determined the answer is ``cannot be determined''\\
seating and ranking & fix the most constrained person first (the middle, the ends) and use ``next to'' and ``right of'' to split cases; position from left $+$ position from right $=n+1$; people between two positions $=|p_1-p_2|-1$\\
syllogisms & Venn diagrams: draw every picture the premises allow and keep the conclusions that hold in \emph{all} of them; apply the exam convention (named sets are non-empty) and the either--or rule below\\
sets and cubes & inclusion--exclusion $|A\cup B|=|A|+|B|-|A\cap B|$ (and its three-set form); a cube $n\times n\times n$ painted outside and cut into unit cubes has 8 corner cubes (3 painted faces), $12(n-2)$ edge cubes (2), $6(n-2)^2$ face cubes (1) and $(n-2)^3$ unpainted ones\\
\bottomrule
\end{xltabular}
\end{center}
\subsection{Syllogisms: two conventions, one program}
Placement papers give two premises and two or three conclusions and ask which \emph{follow}. In strict logic ``All $A$ are $B$'' does not promise that any $A$ exists, so ``Some $B$ are $A$'' does not follow; the usual exam convention instead treats every named set as non-empty, which makes it follow. If the worked example of your test shows ``Some $B$ are $A$'' following from ``All $A$ are $B$'', the exam convention is in force. The program enumerates \emph{every model} of up to five elements (each element belongs to some subset of $A,B,C$) and declares a conclusion to follow only if it holds in all models of the premises; with it the whole two-premise table below is computed, not remembered.
\begin{center}\small
\begin{tabular}{@{}lll@{}}
\toprule
\textbf{Form} & \textbf{Reads} & \textbf{Converts to}\\
\midrule
A (universal affirmative) & All $X$ are $Y$ & Some $Y$ are $X$ (exam convention only), \emph{not} All $Y$ are $X$\\
E (universal negative) & No $X$ is $Y$ & No $Y$ is $X$\\
I (particular affirmative) & Some $X$ are $Y$ & Some $Y$ are $X$\\
O (particular negative) & Some $X$ are not $Y$ & nothing valid\\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
\textbf{Either--or rule.} If neither of two conclusions follows alone but they are complementary (``Some $X$ are $Y$'' with ``No $X$ is $Y$'', or ``All $X$ are $Y$'' with ``Some $X$ are not $Y$''), then exactly one of them is true in every model, and the exam answer is ``either I or II follows''. The output shows this for the example of the mock test.



\Needspace*{5\baselineskip}
\begin{lstlisting}[style=out,basicstyle=\ttfamily\fontsize{7.2}{8.6}\selectfont,title={ap\_logic.py: output}]
A = engineers, B = graduates, C = artists.  Premises: All A are B.  Some B are C.
  conclusion                        strict logic   exam convention (sets non-empty)
  Some engineers are artists        no             no
  Some artists are graduates        FOLLOWS        FOLLOWS
  No engineer is an artist          no             no
  Some graduates are engineers      no             FOLLOWS
  Some artists are not engineers    no             no
  exactly one of 'Some engineers are artists' / 'No engineer is an artist' holds in every model: True
  -> the exam answer is 'either I or II follows'

what follows about A and C from one premise about A,B and one about B,C:
  premises                           strict logic       extra under the exam convention
  All A are B; All B are C           All A are C        Some A are C
  All A are B; No B is C             No A is C          Some A are not C; Some C are not A
  No A is B; All B are C             -                  Some C are not A
  No A is B; Some B are C            Some C are not A   -
  Some A are B; All B are C          Some A are C       -
  Some A are B; No B is C            Some A are not C   -
  the other 10 of the 16 premise pairs allow no conclusion linking A and C

five people P Q R S T in a row (left to right); rules:
  1. R sits exactly in the middle
  2. P does not sit at an end
  3. Q sits somewhere to the right of R
  4. S sits next to P
  5. T does not sit at an end
arrangements that satisfy all rules: 1 -> ['SPRTQ']
   without rule 1 there would be 8 arrangements
   without rule 2 there would be 2 arrangements
   without rule 3 there would be 2 arrangements
   without rule 4 there would be 2 arrangements
   without rule 5 there would be 2 arrangements

walk 10 m north, right 5 m, right 10 m, left 3 m -> position (8, 0): 8 m east of the start
shift-by-1 code: COMPILER -> DPNQJMFS, so PTX -> QUY;  reverse + shift: NVIDIA -> BJEJWO
blood relation, all small families checked -> ['woman is his mother']
4x4x4 cube painted outside, cut into 64 unit cubes: with 0/1/2/3 painted faces = [8, 24, 24, 8]
   (formulas: (n-2)^3, 6(n-2)^2, 12(n-2), 8)

series [2, 6, 12, 20, 30]         next = 42  (constant after 2 difference row(s))
series [3, 6, 11, 18, 27]         next = 38  (constant after 2 difference row(s))
series [1, 1, 2, 3, 5, 8]         next = 13  (Fibonacci)
series [2, 3, 5, 9, 17]           next = 33  (differences are geometric)
series [3, 8, 6, 13, 9, 18, 12]   next = 23  (interleaved, constant after 1 difference row(s))
series [2, 6, 18, 54]             next = 162 (geometric)
\end{lstlisting}
Notes on the output. (1) In the table only 6 of the 16 premise pairs link $A$ and $C$ at all, which is why a conclusion that merely ``sounds plausible'' (for example ``Some $A$ are $C$'' from ``All $A$ are $B$; Some $B$ are $C$'') is almost always wrong; the middle term must be \emph{distributed} (covered completely) in at least one premise. (2) The seating puzzle has exactly one solution and every rule is needed: dropping any one of them leaves 2 arrangements (8 for the middle-seat rule), so none of the clues is redundant. (3) \texttt{next\_term} tries the patterns in the order you should: geometric, Fibonacci, a constant difference row, geometric differences, interleaving; two equal entries in the last row are required before a polynomial pattern is trusted (four terms always fit \emph{some} cubic, e.g.\ 2, 6, 18, 54 would otherwise give 130 instead of 162).

\section{Classic puzzles, solved by search \prio{2}}
Interviewers use puzzles to watch you \emph{structure} a problem. Almost every classic is one of four shapes: (1) a \textbf{state graph} with a goal (jugs, river crossing, bridge and torch): model the state, then breadth-first search finds the fewest steps and Dijkstra the least cost; (2) an \textbf{information bound} (coins: $w$ weighings have $3^w$ outcomes); (3) an \textbf{optimisation by dynamic programming} (egg drop); (4) an \textbf{invariant or pattern} (100 doors, Josephus). Say which shape it is \emph{before} you start solving; that sentence is half the credit. The code is short because Chapter~\ref{ch:dsa}'s tools (queues, heaps, DP tables) do the work.
\Needspace*{5\baselineskip}
\begin{lstlisting}[language={Python},basicstyle=\ttfamily\fontsize{7.2}{8.6}\selectfont,title={ap\_puzzles.py: the classics as search problems}]
import heapq, itertools, math
from collections import deque

def bridge(times):                          # bridge and torch: cross in pairs, somebody must carry the torch back
    everyone = frozenset(times)
    start = (everyone, 0)                   # (people on the left bank, torch side: 0 = left)
    best, heap, tick = {start: 0}, [(0, 0, start)], 0
    while heap:
        t, _, (left, side) = heapq.heappop(heap)
        if not left: return t
        if t > best.get((left, side), 1e9): continue
        group = left if side == 0 else everyone - left
        for k in (1, 2):
            for move in itertools.combinations(sorted(group), k):
                new_left = left - frozenset(move) if side == 0 else left | frozenset(move)
                nt = t + max(move)                                  # a pair walks at the slower speed
                if nt < best.get((new_left, 1 - side), 1e9):
                    best[(new_left, 1 - side)] = nt
                    tick += 1
                    heapq.heappush(heap, (nt, tick, (new_left, 1 - side)))

def jugs(ca, cb, goal):                     # BFS over (litres in jug A, litres in jug B)
    seen, queue = {(0, 0): None}, deque([(0, 0)])
    while queue:
        a, b = queue.popleft()
        if goal in (a, b):
            path, state = [], (a, b)
            while state is not None: path.append(state); state = seen[state]
            return path[::-1]
        ab, ba = min(a, cb - b), min(b, ca - a)                      # amounts that can be poured A->B and B->A
        for nxt in ((ca, b), (a, cb), (0, b), (a, 0), (a - ab, b + ab), (a + ba, b - ba)):
            if nxt not in seen: seen[nxt] = (a, b); queue.append(nxt)

def river():                                # farmer, wolf, goat, cabbage: 0 = left bank, 1 = right bank
    start, goal = (0, 0, 0, 0), (1, 1, 1, 1)
    safe = lambda s: not ((s[1] == s[2] != s[0]) or (s[2] == s[3] != s[0]))    # nobody is eaten while he is away
    seen, queue = {start: 0}, deque([start])
    while queue:
        s = queue.popleft()
        if s == goal: return seen[s]
        for cargo in (None, 1, 2, 3):                                # the farmer rows alone or with one item
            if cargo is not None and s[cargo] != s[0]: continue
            n = list(s); n[0] = 1 - s[0]
            if cargo is not None: n[cargo] = 1 - s[cargo]
            n = tuple(n)
            if safe(n) and n not in seen: seen[n] = seen[s] + 1; queue.append(n)

def egg_drop(eggs, floors):                 # worst-case number of drops, dynamic programming
    f = [[0] + [10 ** 9] * floors] + [[0] * (floors + 1) for _ in range(eggs)]    # no egg: nothing can be decided
    for e in range(1, eggs + 1):
        for fl in range(1, floors + 1):
            f[e][fl] = 1 + min(max(f[e - 1][k - 1], f[e][fl - k]) for k in range(1, fl + 1))
    return f[eggs][floors]

def open_doors(n):                          # pass k toggles every k-th door
    doors = [False] * (n + 1)
    for k in range(1, n + 1):
        for d in range(k, n + 1, k): doors[d] = not doors[d]
    return [d for d in range(1, n + 1) if doors[d]]

def josephus(n, k):                         # n people in a circle, every k-th is eliminated; returns the survivor
    people, i = list(range(1, n + 1)), 0
    while len(people) > 1:
        i = (i + k - 1) % len(people)
        people.pop(i)
    return people[0]

def josephus_recurrence(n, k):             # J(1) = 0, J(n) = (J(n-1) + k) mod n  (0-based position of the survivor)
    j = 0
    for m in range(2, n + 1): j = (j + k) % m
    return j + 1

if __name__ == "__main__":
    print("bridge and torch, crossing times 1, 2, 5, 10 min: minimum total time =", bridge((1, 2, 5, 10)))
    path = jugs(3, 5, 4)
    print("jugs of 3 L and 5 L, measure 4 L: %d steps; states (a, b): %s" % (len(path) - 1, path))
    print("wolf, goat and cabbage: minimum number of crossings =", river())
    print("two eggs, 100 floors: worst case %d drops (14 + 13 + ... + 1 = 105 >= 100)" % egg_drop(2, 100))
    print("100 doors: open doors are", open_doors(100), "(the perfect squares)")
    print("Josephus n=41, k=3: the survivor stands at position", josephus(41, 3))
    agree = all(josephus(n, k) == josephus_recurrence(n, k) for n in range(1, 200) for k in range(2, 6))
    print("   the recurrence J(n) = (J(n-1) + k) mod n agrees with the simulation for n < 200, k = 2..5:", agree)
    closed = all(josephus(n, 2) == 2 * (n - 2 ** (n.bit_length() - 1)) + 1 for n in range(1, 200))
    print("   for k = 2 the closed form 2(n - 2^floor(log2 n)) + 1 agrees too:", closed, "(n=41 gives 19)")
    def drops(e, floors):                   # smallest t with C(t,1) + ... + C(t,e) >= floors
        t = 0
        while sum(math.comb(t, i) for i in range(1, e + 1)) < floors: t += 1
        return t
    same = all(drops(e, f) == egg_drop(e, f) for e in range(1, 5) for f in range(1, 101))
    print("   egg drop: sum of binomials C(t,i), i <= e, agrees with the DP for e <= 4 eggs, <= 100 floors:", same)
\end{lstlisting}
\Needspace*{5\baselineskip}
\begin{lstlisting}[style=out,basicstyle=\ttfamily\fontsize{7.2}{8.6}\selectfont,title={ap\_puzzles.py: output}]
bridge and torch, crossing times 1, 2, 5, 10 min: minimum total time = 17
jugs of 3 L and 5 L, measure 4 L: 6 steps; states (a, b): [(0, 0), (0, 5), (3, 2), (0, 2), (2, 0), (2, 5), (3, 4)]
wolf, goat and cabbage: minimum number of crossings = 7
two eggs, 100 floors: worst case 14 drops (14 + 13 + ... + 1 = 105 >= 100)
100 doors: open doors are [1, 4, 9, 16, 25, 36, 49, 64, 81, 100] (the perfect squares)
Josephus n=41, k=3: the survivor stands at position 31
   the recurrence J(n) = (J(n-1) + k) mod n agrees with the simulation for n < 200, k = 2..5: True
   for k = 2 the closed form 2(n - 2^floor(log2 n)) + 1 agrees too: True (n=41 gives 19)
   egg drop: sum of binomials C(t,i), i <= e, agrees with the DP for e <= 4 eggs, <= 100 floors: True
\end{lstlisting}
\begin{center}\small
\begin{xltabular}{\textwidth}{@{}L{3.0cm}L{1.7cm}Y@{}}
\toprule
\textbf{Puzzle} & \textbf{Answer} & \textbf{What to say aloud}\\
\midrule\endhead
bridge and torch (1, 2, 5, 10 min) & 17 min & The two slowest must cross \emph{together} once (10 min), so do not waste a fast person on each of them: 1+2 cross (2), 1 back (1), 5+10 cross (10), 2 back (2), 1+2 cross (2). The ``escort'' strategy with the fastest costs 19.\\
jugs 3 L and 5 L, measure 4 L & 6 steps & The states $(a,b)$ form a graph; BFS gives the shortest path printed above. Because $\gcd(3,5)=1$ every amount from 0 to 5 litres is reachable; with jugs 4 and 6 only even amounts are.\\
wolf, goat, cabbage & 7 crossings & Take the goat over; return; take the wolf (or cabbage); bring the goat \emph{back}; take the cabbage (or wolf); return; take the goat. The goat is the only item that may never be left with either of the others.\\
two eggs, 100 floors & 14 drops & Drop from floor 14; if it breaks scan floors 1--13 upwards, if not go up 13 floors and so on: $14+13+\cdots+1=105\ge100$, and 13 drops would reach only 91. For $e$ eggs and $t$ drops the reachable floors are $\sum_{i=1}^{e}\binom ti$.\\
100 doors & 10 open & Door $d$ is toggled once per divisor of $d$; divisors pair up as $k,d/k$, so the count is odd only when $d$ is a perfect square.\\
Josephus (41, every 3rd) & position 31 & Recurrence $J(1)=0$, $J(n)=(J(n-1)+k)\bmod n$ (0-based position, add 1); for $k=2$ the closed form is $J=2\big(n-2^{\lfloor\log_2n\rfloor}\big)+1$ (rotate the binary representation of $n$ left by one bit), so $J(41)=19$. Both statements are checked in the output.\\
12 coins, heavier or lighter & 3 weighings & There are $12\times2=24$ possibilities and a weighing has three outcomes: $3^2=9<24\le27=3^3$. The first weighing is 4 against 4; if it balances, the odd coin is among the other 4 and the remaining two weighings suffice, otherwise coins move between pans to separate suspected-heavy from suspected-light ones.\\
8 balls, one heavier & 2 weighings & Weigh 3 against 3 (the search found exactly this). If they balance, the heavy ball is one of the other two: weigh them against each other. Otherwise take the heavier three and weigh 1 against 1; if those balance, the third is heavy.\\
\bottomrule
\end{xltabular}
\end{center}
The coin numbers come from the search whose output follows.

\begin{lstlisting}[style=out,basicstyle=\ttfamily\fontsize{7.2}{8.6}\selectfont,title={ap\_coins.py: output}]
12 coins, one is heavier OR lighter: 24 hypotheses; 2 weighings separate at most 3^2 = 9, 3 weighings 27
search: no strategy with fewer than 3 weighings; first weighing of a 3-weighing strategy: (1, 2, 3, 4) vs (5, 6, 7, 8)
simulated on all 24 cases: always correct = True, worst case 3 weighings
8 balls, one heavier: 2 weighings suffice; first weighing (1, 2, 3) vs (4, 5, 6)
\end{lstlisting}
\section{Probability puzzles by enumeration and simulation \prio{2}}
For a probability puzzle, \textbf{enumerate the sample space} if it is small and write down the probability of each outcome; most famous ``paradoxes'' disappear once the outcomes are listed with \emph{their actual probabilities} (including the host's behaviour in Monty Hall). Use simulation only to confirm, and report its standard error.
\Needspace*{5\baselineskip}
\begin{lstlisting}[style=out,basicstyle=\ttfamily\fontsize{7.2}{8.6}\selectfont,title={ap\_prob.py: output}]
Monty Hall: stay wins 1/3, switch wins 2/3
two children: P(both boys | at least one boy) = 1/3;  P(both boys | the elder is a boy) = 1/2
100 prisoners, loop strategy: simulated 0.318 (standard error 0.003), exact 1 - (1/51 + ... + 1/100) = 0.3118
   if everybody opens 50 boxes at random the team wins with probability 0.5^100 = 7.9e-31
expected tosses until HH: 6.0;  until HT: 4.0   (exact: 6 and 4)
expected rolls of a die until all six faces appeared: 14.7   (exact 6 x (1 + 1/2 + ... + 1/6) = 14.7)
\end{lstlisting}
\begin{center}\small
\begin{xltabular}{\textwidth}{@{}L{3.0cm}Y@{}}
\toprule
\textbf{Puzzle} & \textbf{Why}\\
\midrule\endhead
Monty Hall & Your first pick is right with probability $\frac13$ and that does not change when the host opens a goat door: his action is \emph{forced} by the car's position, so all the remaining $\frac23$ moves to the other door.\\
two children & Ordered pairs BB, BG, GB, GG are equally likely. ``At least one boy'' leaves BB, BG, GB, so $\frac13$; ``the elder is a boy'' leaves BB, BG, so $\frac12$. The wording of the condition decides.\\
100 prisoners & Following the chain ``box $\to$ number inside $\to$ that box'' succeeds for everybody iff the random permutation has no cycle longer than 50, probability $1-\sum_{k=51}^{100}\frac1k\approx31\%$, against $2^{-100}$ for independent random guessing.\\
HH versus HT & After the first head, HT is finished by the next tail, and another head leaves you where you were: $E=2+2=4$. HH needs a head right after a head, and a tail throws you back to the very start: $E_0=1+\frac12E_H+\frac12E_0$ and $E_H=1+\frac12E_0$ give $E_0=6$. A pattern whose failure loses all progress waits longer than one whose failure keeps some.\\
coupon collector & To see all $n$ faces expect $nH_n$ rolls: $6\times2.45=14.7$ for a die.\\
ants on a polygon & Two ants meet head-on exactly when neighbours walk in opposite senses, so nobody collides only if all walk the same way round: $\frac2{2^n}$.\\
\bottomrule
\end{xltabular}
\end{center}
\section{Logic puzzles verified by model checking \prio{2}}
The same idea as the syllogism program: list all \emph{worlds} consistent with the puzzle, test each rule in each world, and read off what is true in \emph{all} remaining worlds. This is also exactly the thinking of Chapter~\ref{ch:verif}: a claim is established by checking it on the whole (small) input space, not by examples.
\begin{lstlisting}[language={Python},basicstyle=\ttfamily\fontsize{7.2}{8.6}\selectfont]
import itertools
from fractions import Fraction as F

# ---- knights always tell the truth, knaves always lie: try all 2^n assignments ------------------------------
def knights_and_knaves(claims):             # claims[x](world) = truth of what x says (None: x is silent)
    names, sols = list(claims), []
    for kinds in itertools.product((True, False), repeat=len(names)):          # True = knight
        world = dict(zip(names, kinds))
        if all(claims[x] is None or claims[x](world) == world[x] for x in names): sols.append(world)
    return [" ".join("%s=%s" % (k, "knight" if v else "knave") for k, v in w.items()) for w in sols]

print("A: 'B is a knave'; B: 'A and C are the same kind'; C: 'A is a knave'")
print("   ->", knights_and_knaves({"A": lambda w: not w["B"], "B": lambda w: w["A"] == w["C"],
                                   "C": lambda w: not w["A"]}))
print("A: 'B is a knave'; B: 'A and I are the same kind'")
print("   ->", knights_and_knaves({"A": lambda w: not w["B"], "B": lambda w: w["A"] == w["B"]}))
print("A: 'we are both knaves' (B says nothing)")
print("   ->", knights_and_knaves({"A": lambda w: not w["A"] and not w["B"], "B": None}))

# ---- two guards, one always lies; ask: "which door would the OTHER guard name as the way to heaven?" --------
def guard_answers_hell_door():
    verdicts = set()
    for liar, heaven in itertools.product((0, 1), repeat=2):               # which guard lies; which door is heaven
        name_heaven = lambda g: (1 - heaven) if g == liar else heaven       # what guard g says when asked directly
        for asked in (0, 1):
            reported = name_heaven(1 - asked)                                # what the other guard would say
            answer = (1 - reported) if asked == liar else reported           # reported truthfully, or the opposite
            verdicts.add(answer == 1 - heaven)                               # does the answer name the HELL door?
    return verdicts
print("two guards, 4 worlds x 2 guards asked: the answer is always the door to hell:", guard_answers_hell_door(),
      "-> take the other door")

# ---- hats in a line: the back person announces the parity of the hats he sees ------------------------------
def survivors(colours):                     # colours[0] stands at the back and sees everybody in front of him
    announced = sum(colours[1:]) % 2        # his own guess, chosen so that the others can decode the parity
    guesses = [announced]
    for i in range(1, len(colours)):        # person i heard i-1 correct answers and sees the hats in front of him
        guesses.append((announced - sum(guesses[1:i]) - sum(colours[i + 1:])) % 2)
    return sum(g == c for g, c in zip(guesses, colours))
counts = [survivors(c) for c in itertools.product((0, 1), repeat=10)]
print("10 prisoners, black/white hats, all 1024 colourings: %d to %d survive (the back person gambles)"
      % (min(counts), max(counts)))

# ---- ants on the corners of an n-gon: each walks along an edge, clockwise or anticlockwise --------------------
def no_collision(n):                        # head-on meeting: ant i goes clockwise and ant i+1 anticlockwise
    ok = sum(1 for d in itertools.product((0, 1), repeat=n)
             if not any(d[i] == 1 and d[(i + 1) % n] == 0 for i in range(n)))
    return F(ok, 2 ** n)
print("ants on an n-gon, P(nobody collides):", ", ".join("n=%d: %s" % (n, no_collision(n)) for n in (3, 4, 5, 6)))
print("   (= 2/2^n: all of them must walk the same way round)")

# ---- one poisoned bottle among 1000, test strips read after an hour: binary code ----------------------------
def poison_decoded_correctly(bottles=1000, strips=10):
    for bad in range(bottles):
        dead = [i for i in range(strips) if (bad >> i) & 1]      # strip i touches the bottles whose bit i is set
        if sum(1 << i for i in dead) != bad: return False        # the pattern of dead strips spells the bottle number
    return True
print("1000 bottles, one poisoned, 10 strips as a binary code: decoded correctly for every bottle:",
      poison_decoded_correctly())
print("   (2^10 = 1024 >= 1000)")

# ---- Cheryl's birthday: reasoning about what the other person knows is just filtering ------------------------
DATES = [("May", 15), ("May", 16), ("May", 19), ("June", 17), ("June", 18),
         ("July", 14), ("July", 16), ("Aug", 14), ("Aug", 15), ("Aug", 17)]
def same(ds, i, v): return [d for d in ds if d[i] == v]                 # dates with month (i=0) / day (i=1) v
step1 = [d for d in DATES if all(len(same(DATES, 1, e[1])) > 1 for e in same(DATES, 0, d[0]))]
step2 = [d for d in step1 if len(same(step1, 1, d[1])) == 1]
step3 = [d for d in step2 if len(same(step2, 0, d[0])) == 1]
text = lambda ds: ", ".join("%s %d" % d for d in ds)
print("Cheryl's birthday; dates left after Albert's first remark:", text(step1))
print("   after Bernard's 'now I know':", text(step2))
print("   after Albert's 'now I know too':", text(step3))
\end{lstlisting}
\Needspace*{5\baselineskip}
\begin{lstlisting}[style=out,basicstyle=\ttfamily\fontsize{7.2}{8.6}\selectfont,title={ap\_riddles.py: output}]
A: 'B is a knave'; B: 'A and C are the same kind'; C: 'A is a knave'
   -> ['A=knight B=knave C=knave']
A: 'B is a knave'; B: 'A and I are the same kind'
   -> ['A=knight B=knave']
A: 'we are both knaves' (B says nothing)
   -> ['A=knave B=knight']
two guards, 4 worlds x 2 guards asked: the answer is always the door to hell: {True} -> take the other door
10 prisoners, black/white hats, all 1024 colourings: 9 to 10 survive (the back person gambles)
ants on an n-gon, P(nobody collides): n=3: 1/4, n=4: 1/8, n=5: 1/16, n=6: 1/32
   (= 2/2^n: all of them must walk the same way round)
1000 bottles, one poisoned, 10 strips as a binary code: decoded correctly for every bottle: True
   (2^10 = 1024 >= 1000)
Cheryl's birthday; dates left after Albert's first remark: July 14, July 16, Aug 14, Aug 15, Aug 17
   after Bernard's 'now I know': July 16, Aug 15, Aug 17
   after Albert's 'now I know too': July 16
\end{lstlisting}
\begin{center}\small
\begin{xltabular}{\textwidth}{@{}L{3.0cm}Y@{}}
\toprule
\textbf{Puzzle} & \textbf{Answer and the idea}\\
\midrule\endhead
knights and knaves & Try all $2^n$ assignments; a knight's statement must be true and a knave's false. ``We are both knaves'' forces its speaker to be a knave (a knight cannot say it) and then the other must be a knight.\\
two guards & Ask one guard ``which door would the \emph{other} guard name as the way to heaven?'' and take the other door: the liar misreports the truth-teller's (true) answer and the truth-teller truthfully reports the liar's (false) answer, so the reply always names hell.\\
hats in a line & The back person announces the parity of the black hats he sees; everybody else computes their own hat from that parity, the answers already heard and the hats in front. Nine of ten are always right; the first is right with probability $\frac12$.\\
ants on a polygon & Head-on meetings happen when neighbours walk in opposite senses, so only the two ``all the same way'' choices are safe: $\frac2{2^n}$.\\
poisoned bottle & Give each bottle a 10-bit number and let strip $i$ taste every bottle whose bit $i$ is 1; the pattern of strips that turn is the number of the bad bottle. Binary codes are the general answer to ``find one defective item with few tests''.\\
Cheryl's birthday & Each statement removes the dates that would make it false; Albert's first remark leaves July and August, Bernard's ``now I know'' removes 14 July and 14 August (a repeated day), Albert's ``now I know too'' removes August (two dates remain there). Answer: 16 July.\\
\bottomrule
\end{xltabular}
\end{center}
\begin{center}\small
\begin{xltabular}{\textwidth}{@{}L{3.3cm}Y@{}}
\toprule
\textbf{Puzzle (no program needed)} & \textbf{Answer and reasoning}\\
\midrule\endhead
two ropes, each burns in 60 min but unevenly; measure 45 min & Light rope 1 at \emph{both} ends and rope 2 at one end. Rope 1 is gone after exactly 30 min (whatever the burn rate, the two flames together consume the 60 minutes that one flame would need). Now light the other end of rope 2, which has 30 min left on one flame and burns out in 15 more: $30+15=45$.\\
three switches, one bulb, one visit & Switch 1 on for ten minutes, then off; switch 2 on; walk in. Lit $=$ switch 2; off and warm $=$ switch 1; off and cold $=$ switch 3. Use a second physical property (heat) to get a third state.\\
how often do the clock hands overlap? & The relative speed is $5.5^\circ$ per minute, so every $\frac{360}{5.5}=65.45$ minutes: 11 times in 12 hours, 22 in a day (not 24).\\
\bottomrule
\end{xltabular}
\end{center}

\section{Verbal ability, if it appears \prio{1}}
The role description asks for strong C/C++ and Python, not for English, and none of the candidate reports mentions a verbal section; if one appears it is short. Three habits are enough: \textbf{reading comprehension}: read the questions first, skim the passage for their keywords, and eliminate options with absolute words (``always'', ``never'', ``only'') unless the passage says exactly that; \textbf{sentence correction}: check subject--verb agreement (``one of the engineers \emph{is}''), tense consistency, pronoun reference, parallel structure (``reading, writing and \emph{to code}'' is wrong), articles (``an hour'', ``a university'') and prepositions (``different \emph{from}'', ``discuss'' takes no \emph{about}); \textbf{para-jumbles}: find the opening sentence (it introduces the subject without a pronoun), chain the rest by pronouns and connectors (``however'', ``this'', ``also''), and check the chronology. Confusable pairs worth ten seconds: affect/effect, principal/principle, its/it's, complement/compliment, whether/weather.

\section{Mock test: 30 questions in 40 minutes \prio{3}}\label{sec:mock}
\newcommand{\mq}[6]{\par\medskip\noindent\begin{minipage}{\linewidth}\textbf{\color{qviolet}M#1.}\ #2\par\nopagebreak\smallskip\noindent\hspace*{1.4em}\textbf{(A)}~#3\qquad\textbf{(B)}~#4\qquad\textbf{(C)}~#5\qquad\textbf{(D)}~#6\end{minipage}\par}
\textbf{Rules.} Set a timer for \textbf{40 minutes}, use pen and paper only, mark a guess with a star and the questions you skipped with a circle, and do not look at the key. Target: at least 23 correct (75\%), at least 26 if you have the time to revise. The questions are original practice items of the genres above, deliberately a little denser than the 10--15 questions the reports suggest, so that the real section feels light.

\subsection*{Section A: quantitative aptitude (M1--M18)}

\subsection*{Answer key with working}
\begin{center}\small
\begin{xltabular}{\textwidth}{@{}L{0.95cm}L{0.7cm}Y@{}}
\toprule
\textbf{Q} & \textbf{Key} & \textbf{Working}\\
\midrule\endhead
M1 & \textbf{B} & Take cost $=100$: marked $125$, sold at $125\times0.9=112.5$, profit $12.5\%$.\\
M2 & \textbf{C} & LCM method: 36 units; A does 3 and B 2 units a day. Four days of both: $4\times5=20$ units; 16 remain; B needs $16/2=8$ days.\\
M3 & \textbf{D} & Distance $=150+250=400$~m in 20~s $=20$~m/s $=20\times3.6=72$~km/h.\\
M4 & \textbf{A} & Milk 30~L, water 10~L. Milk stays 30, so the water must become $\frac23\cdot30=20$~L: add $10$~L.\\
M5 & \textbf{C} & CI $-$ SI for two years $=P(r/100)^2=5000\times0.01=50$ (interest on the first year's interest, $10\%$ of Rs~500).\\
M6 & \textbf{B} & The last digits of $7^1,7^2,7^3,7^4$ are $7,9,3,1$ (period 4); $123\bmod4=3$, so the third entry: $3$.\\
M7 & \textbf{C} & Each zero needs a factor 5 (2s are plentiful): $\lfloor100/5\rfloor+\lfloor100/25\rfloor=20+4=24$.\\
M8 & \textbf{D} & $360=2^3\cdot3^2\cdot5$, so $(3+1)(2+1)(1+1)=24$.\\
M9 & \textbf{A} & Equal distances: harmonic mean $\frac{2\cdot60\cdot40}{60+40}=48$ (the arithmetic mean 50 is the trap).\\
M10 & \textbf{B} & Favourable: $(2,6),(3,5),(4,4),(5,3),(6,2)$, that is 5 of 36 outcomes.\\
M11 & \textbf{A} & Five letters with L twice and E twice: $\frac{5!}{2!\,2!}=30$.\\
M12 & \textbf{C} & Hour hand: $3\cdot30+40\cdot0.5=110^\circ$; minute hand: $40\cdot6=240^\circ$; difference $130^\circ$ (already below $180^\circ$).\\
M13 & \textbf{A} & $\frac{3x+6}{5x+6}=\frac34\Rightarrow12x+24=15x+18\Rightarrow x=2$: B $=10$, A $=6$ (check: $12:16=3:4$).\\
M14 & \textbf{D} & HCF $\times$ LCM $=$ product: $36y=6\cdot180\Rightarrow y=30$ (and $\gcd(36,30)=6$, $\operatorname{lcm}=180$).\\
M15 & \textbf{D} & Growth: 2021 $+25\%$, 2022 $-10\%$, 2023 $+50\%$, 2024 $+40\%$. The largest \emph{absolute} rise is in 2024 ($+54$), the largest \emph{percentage} rise in 2023.\\
M16 & \textbf{A} & $2^3=8\equiv1\pmod7$, so $2^{100}=(2^3)^{33}\cdot2\equiv2$.\\
M17 & \textbf{B} & $\binom52/\binom82=10/28=5/14$ (the value $25/64$ is for drawing \emph{with} replacement).\\
M18 & \textbf{C} & Bayes: $\frac{0.2\cdot0.05}{0.5\cdot0.02+0.3\cdot0.03+0.2\cdot0.05}=\frac{0.010}{0.029}=\frac{10}{29}$ (the prior $\frac15$ and the bike share $\frac9{29}$ are the traps).\\
M19 & \textbf{B} & Differences $4,6,8,10$ give the next difference $12$: $30+12=42$ (the terms are $n(n+1)$).\\
M20 & \textbf{D} & Two interleaved series: $3,6,9,12$ (+3) in the odd places and $8,13,18,\ldots$ (+5) in the even places; the 8th term is an even place: $18+5=23$ (15 continues the wrong series).\\
M21 & \textbf{A} & Every letter moves one place forward (C$\to$D, O$\to$P, \ldots), so P$\to$Q, T$\to$U, X$\to$Y.\\
M22 & \textbf{D} & Positions: $(0,10)\to(5,10)\to(5,0)\to(8,0)$: he is $8$~m east of the start (on the same horizontal line).\\
M23 & \textbf{A} & I is the converse of the second premise. II fails in a model where the artists lie outside the engineers (engineers $\subset$ graduates, artists among the other graduates).\\
M24 & \textbf{B} & R is in place 3; P and T avoid the ends, so they take places 2 and 4 and S, Q take the ends. Q is right of R, so Q is at place 5, S at place 1, hence S is next to P at place 2: S P R T Q.\\
M25 & \textbf{B} & The only daughter of the woman's mother is the woman herself; so the man's mother is the woman.\\
M26 & \textbf{C} & B is $31-15+1=17$th from the left; between places 12 and 17 stand $17-12-1=4$ students.\\
M27 & \textbf{A} & 1 and 2 cross (2), 1 returns (1), 5 and 10 cross (10), 2 returns (2), 1 and 2 cross (2): $17$. Escorting everybody with the fastest takes 19.\\
M28 & \textbf{C} & Fill 5, pour into 3, empty 3, pour 2 into 3, fill 5, pour into 3: states $(0,5),(3,2),(0,2),(2,0),(2,5),(3,4)$ --- 6 steps (breadth-first search confirms nothing shorter).\\
M29 & \textbf{B} & 24 possibilities (12 coins $\times$ heavier/lighter) exceed the $3^2=9$ outcomes of two weighings but not the $3^3=27$ of three; the search found a 3-weighing strategy that starts with 4 against 4.\\
M30 & \textbf{C} & Drop from floors 14, 27, 39, \ldots (steps $14,13,12,\ldots$): $14+13+\cdots+1=105\ge100$, while 13 drops cover only $91$.\\
\bottomrule
\end{xltabular}
\end{center}

percentages, profit, ratio, mixture, interest, ages & M1, M4, M5, M13 & formula sheet rows ``percentages'' to ``interest''; the trap box\\
speed, trains, work, pipes & M2, M3, M9 & ``averages'', ``speed, distance, time'', ``work and pipes''\\
number system & M6, M7, M8, M14, M16 & divisibility, unit digits, factors; the shortcut list\\
counting, probability & M10, M11, M17, M18 & ``counting'' and ``probability'' rows; the probability puzzles\\
clocks, data interpretation & M12, M15 & ``clocks, calendars''; the five DI rules\\
series, coding, direction, ranking & M19--M22, M26 & the reasoning table; run \texttt{next\_term} on your own series\\

\begin{tipbox}[After the mock test]
Do not redo the questions you got right. For each miss write one line: ``what I should have noticed'' (a trap, a formula, a unit). Re-sit only those questions the next day; if the same \emph{type} appears twice in your misses, that type is your next 20 minutes.
\end{tipbox}
